Noncommutative protori and inductive spectral triples Thanks: The research of R.F. was partially funded by an NSERC Discovery Grant.
Remus Floricel, Patrick Melanson

Abstract
. We study inductive limits of higher-dimensional noncommutative tori, which we call noncommutative protori. We compute the Elliott invariants for broad classes of unital and nonunital systems, including toric maps, Morita-corner embeddings, and dimension-changing and proper embeddings. For the resulting simple limits we determine explicitly the ordered K K -groups, trace cone, scale, and projection scale, yielding concrete classification criteria. We also construct compatible spectral triples and locally compact spectral triples on these limits via Fourier- and Morita-compatible Dirac structures.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

Primary 46L05, 46L35, 58B34;
Secondary 46L55, 19K14

1.
Introduction and Background

Noncommutative tori
[
36
,
38
]
are among the fundamental
examples in noncommutative geometry
[
11
]
and the classification theory of

C^{*}
-algebras. They are sufficiently tractable to admit explicit
computations of
K
-theory, traces, Morita equivalence, and Dirac-type operators, while still rich enough to exhibit
genuinely noncommutative phenomena
[
14
,
9
,
7
,
10
,
20
,
38
,
35
]
.

In this paper, we study inductive limits of such
algebras. More precisely, we consider systems of the form

A_{\mathrm{pro}}\;\cong\;\varinjlim(B_{n},\phi_{n}),\qquad B_{n}:=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n}})\;\cong\;M_{r_{n}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes A_{\Theta_{n}},

where each
A_{\Theta_{n}}
is a simple higher-dimensional noncommutative torus
and the connecting maps
\phi_{n}
are nonzero
*
-homomorphisms. We call such limits noncommutative protori, or protoral
C^{*}
-
algebras. The terminology is motivated by the classical fact that protori
[
25
,
24
]
, that is, compact connected abelian groups, are inverse limits of ordinary tori; after passing to continuous function algebras, inverse limits of spaces become direct limits of commutative
C^{*}
-algebras.

The two-dimensional unital case has already appeared in an important form in
the work of Latrémolière and Packer on noncommutative solenoids

[
26
]
. Their noncommutative solenoids are realized as inductive
limits of irrational rotation algebras, and their
K
-theory and classification
are described explicitly in terms of the solenoid parameter. Subsequent work
has also studied spectral triples on noncommutative solenoids and their metric
aspects

[
18
,
19
]
.
One purpose of the present paper is to place these examples within a broader
higher-dimensional framework. From our point of view, the solenoid examples
arise as the special case in which the building blocks are two-dimensional
rotation algebras and the connecting maps are unital toric maps induced by
scalar integer matrices.

The main novelty of the present work lies in allowing a substantially broader class of connecting maps. In addition to unital toric maps arising from integer lattice transformations, we also consider maps that factor through full Morita corners, maps that change dimension, and same-dimensional embeddings whose image is a proper unital subalgebra of a corner. Consequently, the class studied here encompasses both unital and nonunital inductive systems. The nonunital case is particularly significant, as the trace scale and projection scale become part of the invariant, capturing information that is not visible from the abstract ordered
K
-groups alone.

The first part of the paper studies homomorphisms between simple
noncommutative tori. We use classification results for homomorphisms between
simple tracial rank zero
C^{*}
-algebras
[
28
,
29
]
to formulate
K
-theoretic and
trace-theoretic criteria for the existence and uniqueness, up to approximate
unitary equivalence, of unital and nonunital maps between noncommutative tori
and their matrix amplifications (see Theorems
2.2
and
2.3
). We then identify concrete representatives
among these maps. In particular, we show in Proposition
2.4
that, if
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\Theta\equiv M^{t}\Psi M\pmod{M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}},

then there is a monomial
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\longrightarrow A_{\Psi}
such that

\varphi_{M,z}(U_{j})=z_{j}V^{Me_{j}}.

On
K
-theory this map is given by the exterior powers

\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)
and
\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M)
(see Proposition
2.7
). We also
describe how Morita equivalences and full corners produce nonunital connecting
maps (Theorem
2.10
, Theorem
2.12
, and Corollary
2.13
). These particular
*
-homomorphisms, grouped into what we call Case (1), Case (2), Case (3), and Case (4), provide the basic supply of inductive systems used throughout the paper.

The second part computes the Elliott invariants of these inductive limits. We first show in Proposition
3.1
that
A_{\mathrm{pro}}=\varinjlim(B_{n},\phi_{n})
is simple, separable, nuclear, satisfies the Universal Coefficient Theorem (UCT), and
has real rank zero and stable rank one. Moreover,

K_{i}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\cong\varinjlim\bigl(K_{i}(B_{n}),(\phi_{n})_{*i}\bigr),
and the positive cone on
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
is the corresponding direct-limit cone. Since each
building block has a unique trace, the cone of densely defined lower
semicontinuous traces on
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is one-dimensional. The
scale function is given by
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\sup_{n}c_{n},

and the projection scale is
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[\,0,(\iota_{n})_{*}([1_{B_{n}}])\,]
(see Theorem
3.2
).
These formulas make the Elliott invariant completely explicit for the systems
considered here.

This invariant computation is one of the central points of the paper. It
turns the classification of noncommutative protori into a concrete calculation
with ordered direct limits, trace ranges, trace scales, and projection scales.
In the unital case, the scale is simply the order unit. In the stable case,
the trace scale is infinite and the projection scale is the full positive cone.
In intermediate nonunital cases, however, the projection scale can be a proper
subset of the positive cone, and it can distinguish limits that would otherwise
look similar at the level of
K
-groups and trace pairings. This is why the
nonunital scale data are treated explicitly throughout the paper.

We then work out a series of examples. The unital toric
N
-solenoid recovers
the noncommutative solenoid picture of Latrémolière and Packer as a special
case (see Remark
3.4
). Pure-corner systems give stable noncommutative protori (Example
3.6
). Dimension-changing
systems produce limits whose
K
-groups are exterior algebras on countably
generated lattices (Example
3.7
). Same-dimensional noncorner systems yield nonunital
examples with projection scales governed by strict trace inequalities (Example
3.8
). We also
construct unital systems in which the connecting maps are designed to change

K_{1}
while leaving
K_{0}
fixed (Example
3.9
), and higher-dimensional examples in which
infinitesimal
K_{0}
-classes are killed in the limit (Example
3.10
). These examples illustrate
that inductive limits of noncommutative tori can display behavior that is not
visible at any single finite stage.

The final part of the paper studies spectral triples on noncommutative protori.
In the unital toric case, the connecting maps act directly on Fourier labels.
We first isolate a flexible single-stage construction: a selfadjoint
matrix-valued Fourier multiplier

F\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\mathrm{End}(S)
whose spectrum escapes to infinity and whose
translation increments are bounded gives a spectral triple on
A_{\Theta}

(Proposition
4.1
). This recovers the
usual flat Dirac operators and length-function triples, and it is stable under
compatible bounded perturbations and right-conformal deformations. For an
inductive system, Fourier-compatible data
(F_{n},S_{n},J_{n})
, satisfying the
intertwining relation
F_{n+1}(M_{n}x)J_{n}=J_{n}F_{n}(x)
, produce an inductive system of spectral triples
(Proposition
4.7
), to which we apply
the inductive-limit construction from
[
21
]
. We then give
a convenient direct-limit criterion, formulated on the label group

\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{m_{n}},M_{n})
, ensuring compact resolvent and bounded
commutators for the limit operator (Corollary
4.8
).
The unital
N
-solenoid examples show both a natural flat compatible operator
which fails compact resolvent (Example
4.9
) and a length-type modification which restores
compact resolvent (Example
4.10
); compatible inner fluctuations (Example
4.12
) and right-conformal
deformations (Example
4.14
) are also constructed.

For nonunital systems the situation is different. The connecting maps factor
through full corners and are no longer unital, so the natural limiting objects
are locally compact spectral triples rather than ordinary unital spectral
triples. We formulate Morita-compatible Dirac data using smooth full
projections, smooth corner isomorphisms, trace-rescaled GNS isometries, and
stagewise operators intertwined through the toric, Morita, and corner pieces (see Definition
4.15
).
Theorem
4.16
proves that the resulting
inductive-limit operator has bounded commutators exactly when the finite-stage
commutators are uniformly bounded, and it reduces local compactness to the
compactness of the cut-down resolvents
\pi(\iota_{n}(1_{B_{n}}))(1+D_{\mathrm{pro}}^{2})^{-1/2}
. We verify these criteria in
three model families: the pure-corner stable protorus (Example
4.17
), the dimension-changing
weighted-length model (Example
4.18
), and the same-dimensional noncorner flag-filtration
model (Example
4.19
). These examples explain why additional weights or filtrations are needed:
the limit operator generally cannot have compact resolvent, but local
compactness is recovered after cutting down by finite-stage units.

The importance of these constructions is twofold. On the
C^{*}
-algebraic
side, they provide a computable class of simple classifiable inductive limits
built from noncommutative tori, extending the known solenoid examples to a much
larger range of higher-dimensional and nonunital systems. On the geometric
side, they show that compatible Dirac-type data can be transported through the
same inductive systems, giving spectral and locally compact spectral triples on
the resulting noncommutative protori. Thus the paper connects explicit
Elliott-invariant classification with noncommutative metric geometry in a
single inductive-limit framework.

We conclude this introductory section by briefly recalling the definition and basic structure of simple higher-dimensional noncommutative tori. Additional properties and notation will be introduced later, as needed.

Let
m\geq 2
, and let
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric. The
noncommutative
m
-torus
A_{\Theta}

[
38
]
is the universal
unital
C^{*}
-algebra generated by unitaries

U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
satisfying

(1)

U_{k}U_{j}=e^{2\pi i\Theta_{j,k}}\,U_{j}U_{k},\qquad 1\leq j,k\leq m.

It is sometimes convenient to use the twisted-group picture. For

x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, set
U^{x}:=U_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots U_{m}^{x_{m}}.

Then the relations (
1
) imply

(2)

U^{y}U^{x}=\exp\bigl(2\pi i\langle x,\Theta y\rangle\bigr)\,U^{x}U^{y},\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

Equivalently,
A_{\Theta}\cong C^{\ast}(\mathbb{Z}^{m},\omega_{\Theta}),

where, for the convention

\lambda_{x}\lambda_{y}=\omega_{\Theta}(x,y)\lambda_{x+y}
, one may take the
normalized
2
-cocycle

\omega_{\Theta}(x,y)=\exp\left(2\pi i\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq m}\Theta_{j,k}x_{k}y_{j}\right).

This cocycle is cohomologous to the alternating cocycle

\sigma_{\Theta}(x,y)=\exp\bigl(-\pi i\langle x,\Theta y\rangle\bigr).

We say that
\Theta
is nondegenerate
[
40
]
if the only

x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
satisfying
\exp(2\pi i\langle x,\Theta y\rangle)=1

for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}

is
x=0
.

Theorem 1.1

(Phillips)
.

If
\Theta
is nondegenerate, then
A_{\Theta}
is a simple AT algebra with
real rank zero and stable rank one. It has a unique tracial state, denoted

\tau_{\Theta}
. In particular,
A_{\Theta}
is separable, nuclear, satisfies
the UCT, and has tracial rank zero. Moreover,

(3)

K_{0}(A_{\Theta})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{m},\qquad K_{1}(A_{\Theta})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{m},

so both
K
-groups are free abelian of rank
2^{m-1}
.

Proof.

For nondegenerate
\Theta
, the algebra
A_{\Theta}
is simple and has at
most one tracial state by
[
40
, Theorem 3.7 and Lemma 3.1]
. The
canonical trace on the dense Fourier
*
-subalgebra gives existence. The AT
and real rank zero assertions are
[
32
, Theorem 3.8]
. Stable rank
one and tracial rank zero follow from the structure of simple AT algebras with
real rank zero. Since
A_{\Theta}
is a twisted group
C^{*}
-algebra of

\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, it is separable, nuclear, and satisfies the UCT. Finally, the

K
-theory identifications in (
3
) follow from repeated use
of the Pimsner–Voiculescu exact sequence, as recalled in

[
32
,
37
]
.
∎

For nondegenerate
\Theta
, the order on
K_{0}(A_{\Theta})
is determined by
the unique trace. Write

\rho_{\Theta}:=(\tau_{\Theta})_{*}\colon K_{0}(A_{\Theta})\to\mathbb{R}

for the trace pairing. Elliott computed
\rho_{\Theta}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta}))

explicitly in terms of Pfaffians of even-dimensional submatrices of

\Theta

[
13
]
. For our purposes, the key point is that the positive
cone is trace-determined
[
37
, Theorem 6.1]
:

(4)

K_{0}(A_{\Theta})^{+}=\{x\in K_{0}(A_{\Theta}):\rho_{\Theta}(x)>0\}\cup\{0\}.

2.
Homomorphisms of simple noncommutative tori

In this section we separate two issues:

(1)

the classification of all homomorphisms between simple noncommutative tori up to approximate unitary equivalence, and

(2)

the construction of concrete monomial representatives coming from integer matrices.

The first point is an application of Phillips’s structure theorem for simple higher-dimensional noncommutative tori together with Lin’s classification of unital monomorphisms from AH-algebras into simple tracial-rank-zero algebras
[
29
]
. The second point recovers the toric maps that are useful for explicit constructions.

2.1.
The general classification

We begin by recalling the construction of the Dadarlat–Loring
KL
-group
KL(A,B)
; see

[
12
]
.
Let
A
and
B
be separable
C^{*}
-algebras, with

A
satisfying the UCT. The UCT yields the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(K_{*}(A),K_{*+1}(B)\bigr)\longrightarrow KK(A,B)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(K_{*}(A),K_{*}(B)\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.

The group
KL(A,B)
is defined by

KL(A,B):=KK(A,B)/\operatorname{Pext}\bigl(K_{*}(A),K_{*+1}(B)\bigr),

where
\operatorname{Pext}
denotes the subgroup of pure extension classes in
the UCT kernel. Thus every class
\alpha\in KL(A,B)
induces a graded homomorphism

\alpha_{*}=(\alpha_{*0},\alpha_{*1})\colon K_{*}(A)\to K_{*}(B).

Conversely, for noncommutative tori, we have the following result.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\Theta
and
\Psi
be nondegenerate skew-symmetric matrices. Then every graded group homomorphism
\kappa\colon K_{*}(A_{\Theta})\to K_{*}(A_{\Psi})

determines a unique class in
KL(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})
. The same holds with

A_{\Psi}
replaced by any nonzero corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.1
, the algebras
A_{\Theta}
and
A_{\Psi}
are
separable, nuclear, and satisfy the UCT. Moreover,
K_{*}(A_{\Theta})=K_{0}(A_{\Theta})\oplus K_{1}(A_{\Theta})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\oplus\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{m}
as a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded group. In particular,
K_{*}(A_{\Theta})
is free
abelian. Hence
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(K_{*}(A_{\Theta}),K_{*+1}(A_{\Psi})\bigr)=0,

and the UCT identifies
KK(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(K_{*}(A_{\Theta}),K_{*}(A_{\Psi})\bigr).

Thus every graded homomorphism
\kappa
determines a unique
KK
-class, and
hence a unique
KL
-class.

Now let
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
be a nonzero corner. Since
A_{\Psi}
is simple,

M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
is simple, and every nonzero projection

p\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
is full. Therefore
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
is Morita equivalent
to
A_{\Psi}
. In particular, it is separable, nuclear, satisfies the UCT, and
has the same
K
-theory up to the usual Morita identification. Applying the
same UCT argument with
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
in place of
A_{\Psi}
proves the
corner case.
∎

The following result classifies unital
*
-homomorphisms between simple
higher-dimensional noncommutative tori up to approximate unitary equivalence. To this end, recall that if
A
and
B
are unital
C^{*}
-algebras, then two
*
-homomorphisms

\phi,\psi\colon A\to B
are said to be approximately unitarily equivalent,
written
\phi\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}\psi
, if there exists a sequence of unitaries

u_{n}\in B
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{n}\phi(a)u_{n}^{*}-\psi(a)\|=0

for all
a\in A.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be nondegenerate
skew-symmetric matrices. Let
\kappa=(\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1})\colon K_{*}(A_{\Theta})\to K_{*}(A_{\Psi})

be a graded group homomorphism. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a unital
*
-homomorphism

\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}
with

\phi_{*i}=\kappa_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

(ii)

The
K_{0}
-map satisfies

(5)

\kappa_{0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=[1_{A_{\Psi}}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{\Psi}\circ\kappa_{0}=\rho_{\Theta}.

Moreover, if
\phi,\psi\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}
are unital

*
-homomorphisms, then
\phi\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}\psi

if and only if
\phi_{*0}=\psi_{*0}

and
\phi_{*1}=\psi_{*1}.

Consequently,
\mathrm{Hom}_{1}(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})/\!\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}

is naturally in bijection with the set of graded homomorphisms

\kappa
satisfying (
5
).

Proof.

Assume (i). Since
A_{\Theta}
and
A_{\Psi}
have unique tracial states,
\tau_{\Psi}\circ\phi
is a tracial state on
A_{\Theta}
and hence equals
\tau_{\Theta}
. Passing to
K_{0}
gives
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\phi_{\ast 0}=\rho_{\Theta}.

Since
\phi
is unital,
\phi_{\ast 0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=[1_{A_{\Psi}}]
. Thus (ii) holds.

Now assume (ii). By Lemma
2.1
, the graded homomorphism
\kappa
determines a unique class, still denoted
\kappa
, in
KL(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})
. The trace identity in (
5
), and (
4
), imply that
\kappa_{0}
is strictly positive: if
x\in K_{0}(A_{\Theta})^{+}\setminus\{0\}
, then
\rho_{\Theta}(x)>0
, hence
\rho_{\Psi}(\kappa_{0}(x))=\rho_{\Theta}(x)>0
. Therefore
\kappa_{0}(x)\in K_{0}(A_{\Psi})^{+}\setminus\{0\}
by (
4
).

Define a unital strictly positive linear map
\gamma\colon(A_{\Theta})_{\mathrm{sa}}\to\operatorname{Aff}(T(A_{\Psi}))\cong\mathbb{R}

by
\gamma(a)(\tau_{\Psi}):=\tau_{\Theta}(a).
Since
A_{\Theta}
is simple and
\tau_{\Theta}
is faithful,
\gamma
is strictly positive: if
0\neq a\in(A_{\Theta})_{+}
, then
\gamma(a)(\tau_{\Psi})=\tau_{\Theta}(a)>0.
Moreover,
\gamma
is compatible with
\kappa_{0}
, because
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\kappa_{0}=\rho_{\Theta}.
Thus Lin’s existence theorem for unital monomorphisms from AH algebras into simple
C^{\ast}
-algebras of tracial rank zero applies, and yields a unital monomorphism
\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}

with
[\phi]=\kappa
in
KL(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})
and
\tau_{\Psi}\circ\phi=\tau_{\Theta}

[
29
, Theorem 5.2]
.
Since
K_{*}(A_{\Theta})
is free, the
KL
-class determines the ordinary
graded
K
-theory map, and hence
\phi_{\ast i}=\kappa_{i}
for
i=0,1
. This proves (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).

For uniqueness, let
\phi,\psi\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}
be unital and assume that they induce the same maps on
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
. By Lemma
2.1
, they determine the same class in
KL(A_{\Theta},A_{\Psi})
. Since both maps are unital and the traces are unique, both satisfy
\tau_{\Psi}\circ\phi=\tau_{\Theta}=\tau_{\Psi}\circ\psi.

Therefore Lin’s uniqueness theorem gives
\phi\sim_{\mathrm{au}}\psi

[
29
, Theorem 3.1]
. The converse implication is immediate because approximate unitary equivalence preserves the induced maps on
K
-theory.∎

We now classify all maps into matrix amplifications, which automatically includes the case of
nonunital maps into
A_{\Psi}
itself by taking
k=1
. For
k\geq 1
we identify
K_{i}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))\cong K_{i}(A_{\Psi})

via the standard Morita equivalence, so that
[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]=k[1_{A_{\Psi}}]

in
K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, and we write
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}:=\mathrm{Tr}_{k}\otimes\tau_{\Psi}
for the canonical
unnormalized trace on
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be nondegenerate skew-symmetric matrices, and let
k\geq 1
.
Let
\kappa=(\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1})\colon K_{*}(A_{\Theta})\to K_{*}(A_{\Psi})

be a graded group homomorphism. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a
*
-homomorphism
\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

such that
\phi_{*i}=\kappa_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

(ii)

Either
\kappa_{0}=0
and
\kappa_{1}=0
, corresponding to the zero
homomorphism, or, setting
\eta:=\kappa_{0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])
,
one has

0<\eta\leq k[1_{A_{\Psi}}]
in the ordered group
K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, and
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\kappa_{0}=\rho_{\Psi}(\eta)\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Moreover, if
\phi,\psi\colon A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
are nonzero
*
-homomorphisms, then
\phi\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}\psi

if and only if
\phi_{*0}=\psi_{*0}
and

\phi_{*1}=\psi_{*1}.

Consequently,
\mathrm{Hom}(A_{\Theta},M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))/\!\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}

is the disjoint union of the zero class and the classes parametrized by graded
homomorphisms satisfying the nonzero alternative in
(ii)
.

Proof.

Assume first that (i) holds, and let
\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
be such a map.
If
\phi=0
, then
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{1}=0
and we are done. So suppose
\phi\neq 0
.
Because
A_{\Theta}
is simple,
\phi
is injective. Set
p:=\phi(1_{A_{\Theta}})\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.
Then
p
is a nonzero projection, and
\phi
may be regarded as a unital monomorphism
\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\to pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Moreover,
\eta:=\kappa_{0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=[p]
. Since
0\neq p\leq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, we have
0<\eta\leq[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]=k[1_{A_{\Psi}}]

in
K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
.

The algebra
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
is simple, has tracial rank zero, stable rank one,
and has a unique tracial state. Since
p\neq 0
, the corner

pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
is full and simple. Tracial rank zero passes to unital corners,
and the normalized restriction of the unique trace on
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
is the
unique tracial state on
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
:

\tau_{p}(x):=\frac{\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(x)}{\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)},\qquad x\in pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

Since
\tau_{p}\circ\phi
is a tracial state on
A_{\Theta}
, uniqueness of the trace on
A_{\Theta}
gives
\tau_{p}\circ\phi=\tau_{\Theta}.

Let
t:=\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)=\rho_{\Psi}([p])=\rho_{\Psi}(\eta)>0.

Multiplying the identity
\tau_{p}\circ\phi=\tau_{\Theta}
by
t
gives
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}\circ\phi=t\,\tau_{\Theta}.

Under the standard identification
K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))\cong K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, the map on

K_{0}
induced by the unnormalized trace
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}
is precisely

\rho_{\Psi}
. Hence, passing to
K_{0}
gives
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\kappa_{0}=t\,\rho_{\Theta}=\rho_{\Psi}(\eta)\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Thus (ii) holds.

Conversely, assume (ii). If
\kappa_{0}=0
and
\kappa_{1}=0
, then the zero homomorphism
A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

realizes
\kappa
.

Now assume that
\eta:=\kappa_{0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])

satisfies
0<\eta\leq[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]=k[1_{A_{\Psi}}]

and that
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\kappa_{0}=\rho_{\Psi}(\eta)\rho_{\Theta}.
Set
t:=\rho_{\Psi}(\eta).

By the order description of
K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, the inequality
0<\eta
implies
t>0
.

Since
\eta\in K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))^{+}
, choose a projection
q\in M_{\ell}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))

with
[q]=\eta
. Since
[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]-\eta\in K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))^{+}
, choose a projection

r\in M_{s}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))
with
[r]=[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]-\eta
. Thus
[q]+[r]=[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}]

in
K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))
. Since
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
has stable rank one, projections over
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

satisfy cancellation. Hence equality of
K_{0}
-classes of projections implies
Murray–von Neumann equivalence after identifying projections in matrix
amplifications. Applying this to
[q]+[r]=[1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}],

we obtain that
q\oplus r
is Murray–von Neumann equivalent, in the stabilization,
to
1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
. Therefore
q
is Murray–von Neumann subequivalent to

1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
. Thus
q
is equivalent to a projection
p\leq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}.

Since
p\leq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, we may regard
p
as a projection in

M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, and
[p]=\eta
. Moreover, since
\eta>0
, the projection
p
is nonzero and, since
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

is simple, every nonzero projection in
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, in particular
p
, is full.

Let
\iota\colon pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\hookrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

be the corner inclusion. Since
p
is full,
\iota
induces isomorphisms
\iota_{*i}\colon K_{i}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))\cong K_{i}(A_{\Psi})
for
i=0,1.
Define

\kappa_{i}^{p}:=(\iota_{*i})^{-1}\circ\kappa_{i}\colon K_{i}(A_{\Theta})\to K_{i}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p).

Then
\kappa_{0}^{p}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=[1_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}]=[p].

Notice that the unique tracial state on
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
is

\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}(x)=\frac{\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(x)}{t},\qquad x\in pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p,

and let

\rho_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}:=(\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p})_{*}\colon K_{0}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)\to\mathbb{R}.

For every
x\in K_{0}(A_{\Theta})
, we have
\rho_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}(\kappa_{0}^{p}(x))=\frac{1}{t}\rho_{\Psi}(\kappa_{0}(x))=\frac{1}{t}\,t\,\rho_{\Theta}(x)=\rho_{\Theta}(x).

Thus

(6)

\rho_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}\circ\kappa_{0}^{p}=\rho_{\Theta}.

The ordered
K_{0}
-group of
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
is transported from that of
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
by the full-corner Morita equivalence. Since the positive cone of
K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))
is determined by the trace, the same is true for
K_{0}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)
:

K_{0}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)^{+}=\{y\in K_{0}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p):\rho_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}(y)>0\}\cup\{0\}.

Therefore (
6
) implies that
\kappa_{0}^{p}
is positive and sends nonzero positive classes to nonzero positive classes.

By Lemma
2.1
, the graded homomorphism
\kappa^{p}=(\kappa_{0}^{p},\kappa_{1}^{p})

determines a unique class in
KL(A_{\Theta},pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p).

Define the trace map
\lambda\colon T(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)\to T(A_{\Theta})

by
\lambda(\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p})=\tau_{\Theta}.
Since both trace spaces are singletons, this is the unique affine map between
them. It is strictly positive because
\tau_{\Theta}
is faithful.
The compatibility between
\lambda
and
\kappa^{p}
is exactly
(
6
). Hence Lin’s existence theorem
[
29
, Theorem 5.2]
applies and gives a unital monomorphism

\phi_{p}\colon A_{\Theta}\to pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

with
(\phi_{p})_{*i}=\kappa_{i}^{p}
for
i=0,1
,
and
\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}\circ\phi_{p}=\tau_{\Theta}.
Finally, define

\phi:=\iota\circ\phi_{p}\colon A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi}).

Then
\phi_{*i}=\iota_{*i}\circ(\phi_{p})_{*i}=\iota_{*i}\circ\kappa_{i}^{p}=\kappa_{i}

for
i=0,1
. This proves (i).

It remains to prove the uniqueness statement. Let
\phi,\psi\colon A_{\Theta}\to M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

be nonzero
*
-homomorphisms with
\phi_{*0}=\psi_{*0}
and
\phi_{*1}=\psi_{*1}.
Set
p:=\phi(1_{A_{\Theta}}),

q:=\psi(1_{A_{\Theta}}).

Then
[p]=\phi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=\psi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])=[q]

in
K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))
. Since
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
has stable rank one, cancellation holds for projections.
Thus
[p]=[q]
implies that
p
and
q
are Murray–von Neumann
equivalent. Applying the same argument to
1-p
and
1-q
, we obtain a
unitary
w\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
such that
wpw^{*}=q.

After replacing
\psi
by
\operatorname{Ad}(w^{*})\circ\psi
, we may assume
that
p=q
. Thus both
\phi
and
\psi
are unital monomorphisms
A_{\Theta}\to pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

The inclusion
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\hookrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
induces an isomorphism on
K
-theory, and
\phi
and
\psi
induce the same maps on
K_{0}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)
and
K_{1}(pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p)
. By Lemma
2.1
, they determine the same class in
KL(A_{\Theta},pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p).

The corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
has a unique tracial state, so
\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}\circ\phi=\tau_{\Theta}=\tau_{pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p}\circ\psi.

Lin’s uniqueness theorem therefore gives approximate unitary equivalence of
\phi
and
\psi
inside the corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
. If
v_{n}
are the implementing unitaries in
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
, then
v_{n}+(1-p)

are unitaries in
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, so the same approximate unitary equivalence holds inside
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
. Undoing the initial unitary conjugacy, we obtain
\phi\sim_{\mathrm{a.u.e.}}\psi

as maps into
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.

The converse is immediate, since approximate unitary equivalence preserves the induced maps on
K
-theory.
∎

2.2.
Toric
\ast
–homomorphisms from integer lattice maps

We now turn to concrete lattice-induced maps. They provide explicit representatives for special subclasses of the homomorphisms classified above. Besides the unital monomial maps of Case (1), discussed in Proposition
2.4
and the Morita-corner models of Case (2), treated in Proposition
2.10
, we also record two further geometric families that will be used later: embeddings with dimension change, described in Theorem
2.12
, and same-dimensional embeddings whose range is a proper unital subalgebra of a corner, described in Corollary
2.13
. All four families fit within the complete approximate-unitary-equivalence classification established in Theorems
2.2
and
2.3
.

Proposition 2.4

(Case (1): monomial (toric) homomorphisms)
.

Let
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric matrices.
Let
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
and let
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
.
Assume

(7)

\Theta\equiv M^{t}\Psi M\pmod{M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}},

meaning that
\Theta-M^{t}\Psi M
is an integer-valued skew-symmetric matrix. Then there is a unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\longrightarrow A_{\Psi}

defined on generators by

(8)

\varphi_{M,z}(U_{j})=z_{j}\,V^{Me_{j}}=z_{j}\,V_{1}^{M_{1j}}\cdots V_{n}^{M_{nj}},\qquad 1\leq j\leq m,

where
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
are the canonical generators of
A_{\Psi}
and
e_{j}
is the
j
th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Conversely, any
*
-homomorphism
\varphi\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}
satisfying (
8
) for some
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
and
z\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
forces (
7
).

Proof.

Set
W_{j}:=z_{j}V^{Me_{j}}
.
It suffices to compute the commutation relations among the ordered monomials
V^{Me_{j}}
.
Applying (
2
) in
A_{\Psi}
with
x=Me_{j}
and
y=Me_{k}
gives

V^{Me_{k}}V^{Me_{j}}=\exp\bigl(2\pi i\langle Me_{j},\Psi Me_{k}\rangle\bigr)\,V^{Me_{j}}V^{Me_{k}}=\exp\bigl(2\pi i(M^{t}\Psi M)_{j,k}\bigr)\,V^{Me_{j}}V^{Me_{k}}.

Condition (
7
) is exactly the statement that
\exp(2\pi i\Theta_{j,k})=\exp\bigl(2\pi i(M^{t}\Psi M)_{j,k}\bigr)

for all
j,k
. Therefore the unitaries
W_{1},\dots,W_{m}
satisfy the defining relations (
1
) of
A_{\Theta}
, and the universal property yields a unique unital
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{M,z}
with
\varphi_{M,z}(U_{j})=W_{j}
.

Conversely, if (
8
) holds, then the images satisfy
\varphi(U_{k})\varphi(U_{j})=\exp\bigl(2\pi i(M^{t}\Psi M)_{j,k}\bigr)\,\varphi(U_{j})\varphi(U_{k}),

while the defining relations in
A_{\Theta}
force
\varphi(U_{k})\varphi(U_{j})=\exp(2\pi i\Theta_{j,k})\,\varphi(U_{j})\varphi(U_{k}).

Hence

\exp(2\pi i\Theta_{j,k})=\exp\bigl(2\pi i(M^{t}\Psi M)_{j,k}\bigr)\qquad\text{for all }j,k,

which is equivalent to (
7
).
∎

In preparation for the lemma below, we recall the following standard terminology; see

[
3
,
43
]
. A
C([0,1])
-algebra
is a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a unital
*
-homomorphism
C([0,1])\longrightarrow Z(M(\mathcal{A})),

where
Z(M(\mathcal{A}))
denotes the center of the multiplier algebra of

\mathcal{A}
. Thus elements of
C([0,1])
act on
\mathcal{A}
by central
multipliers. We write
s\in C([0,1])
,
s(a)=a,

for the identity function, viewed as a central self-adjoint multiplier. For

a\in[0,1]
, let
C_{a}([0,1])=\{f\in C([0,1]):f(a)=0\}.

The fiber of
\mathcal{A}
at
a
is
\mathcal{A}_{a}:=\mathcal{A}/\overline{C_{a}([0,1])\mathcal{A}},

and the quotient map is denoted by
\mathrm{ev}_{a}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{a}.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
H\in M_{r}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathrm{skew}}
be a skew-symmetric matrix. Let
\mathcal{A}_{H}
denote the universal

C([0,1])
-algebra generated by unitaries
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{r}

satisfying the relations

(9)

\mathcal{U}_{\ell}\mathcal{U}_{j}=\exp(2\pi i\,sH_{j,\ell})\,\mathcal{U}_{j}\mathcal{U}_{\ell},\qquad 1\leq j,\ell\leq r.

where
s\in C([0,1])
is the identity function
s(a)=a
.
For
a\in[0,1]
, let
\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H}\colon\mathcal{A}_{H}\longrightarrow A_{aH}

be the evaluation map. Then
(\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H})_{*}
is an isomorphism on
K
-theory for
every
a\in[0,1]
. Hence the maps

\mu_{H}:=(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{H})_{*}\circ\bigl((\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{H})_{*}\bigr)^{-1}\colon K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{r}))\longrightarrow K_{*}(A_{H})

identifies
K_{*}(A_{H})
, as a graded abelian group, with
\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{r}\oplus\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{r}

under the standard identification
K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{r}))\cong\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{r}
.

Moreover, let
G\in M_{N}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathrm{skew}}
and let

L\in M_{N\times r}(\mathbb{Z})
satisfy
H=L^{t}GL.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{N}
be the universal generators of

\mathcal{A}_{G}
. Then the assignment

\mathcal{U}_{j}\longmapsto\mathcal{V}^{Le_{j}}=\mathcal{V}_{1}^{L_{1j}}\cdots\mathcal{V}_{N}^{L_{Nj}},\qquad 1\leq j\leq r,

defines a
C([0,1])
-linear
*
-homomorphism
\Phi_{L}\colon\mathcal{A}_{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{G}.

If
f_{L}\colon\mathbb{T}^{N}\to\mathbb{T}^{r}
is defined as

f_{L}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{N})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N}t_{i}^{L_{ij}}\right)_{j=1}^{r},

then the identity
(\varphi_{L,1})_{*}\circ\mu_{H}=\mu_{G}\circ(f_{L}^{*})_{*}
holds on
K
-theory.

Proof.

We notice that in our notation,
\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H}\colon\mathcal{A}_{H}\longrightarrow A_{aH}
, is simply the quotient map to the fiber
(\mathcal{A}_{H})_{a}
at
a
, obtained by identifying
(\mathcal{A}_{H})_{a}
and
A_{aH}
: after evaluating
s
at
a
, the images of the generators satisfy exactly
the defining relations of
A_{aH}
. Hence the universal property of

A_{aH}
gives a surjective map
A_{aH}\to(\mathcal{A}_{H})_{a}
, while the
quotient map
\mathcal{A}_{H}\to A_{aH}
induced by evaluation gives the inverse
map. Thus
(\mathcal{A}_{H})_{a}\cong A_{aH}
.

We first prove that the evaluation maps induce
K
-theory isomorphisms.
This is the usual homotopy-invariance part of the iterated Pimsner–Voiculescu
computation, but we spell out the argument.

For
q=1,\ldots,r
, let
\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(q)}
be the

C([0,1])
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{q}
with
the corresponding relations coming from the upper-left
q\times q
corner of

H
. Thus
\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(1)}\cong C([0,1]\times\mathbb{T}).

For
q\geq 2
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(q)}\cong\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(q-1)}\rtimes_{\alpha_{q}}\mathbb{Z},

where the crossed-product convention is that the implementing unitary
u

satisfies
uau^{*}=\alpha_{q}(a)
, and
\alpha_{q}(\mathcal{U}_{j})=\exp(2\pi i\,sH_{j,q})\,\mathcal{U}_{j},
for all
1\leq j<q.
Evaluation at
a\in[0,1]
intertwines this crossed-product decomposition with
the corresponding crossed-product decomposition
A_{aH}^{(q)}\cong A_{aH}^{(q-1)}\rtimes_{\alpha_{q}^{(a)}}\mathbb{Z}
of
A_{aH}^{(q)}
, where
\alpha_{q}^{(a)}(U_{j})=\exp(2\pi i\,aH_{j,q})U_{j}.

Set
B=\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(q-1)},

C=A_{aH}^{(q-1)},

\beta=\alpha_{q},

\gamma=\alpha_{q}^{(a)},

and let
\varepsilon=\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H,q-1}\colon B\to C.

Then
\varepsilon\circ\beta=\gamma\circ\varepsilon,

so
\varepsilon
induces a
*
-homomorphism
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\colon B\rtimes_{\beta}\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow C\rtimes_{\gamma}\mathbb{Z}.

By naturality of the Pimsner–Voiculescu six-term exact sequence

[
33
]
, we obtain the following commutative diagram, written
in unrolled six-term form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(B\rtimes_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(B\rtimes_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(C\rtimes_{\gamma}\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}(C\rtimes_{\gamma}\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(C).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{B})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\beta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{B})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\gamma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{C})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\gamma_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{C})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{C}}

For
q=1
, the evaluation map
C([0,1]\times\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow C(\mathbb{T})

is a homotopy equivalence and hence induces an isomorphism on
K
-theory.
Assume the result known for
q-1
. Then
\varepsilon_{*}
is an isomorphism
on
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
. The diagram above and the five lemma imply that
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{*}\colon K_{*}(B\rtimes_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow K_{*}(C\rtimes_{\gamma}\mathbb{Z})

is an isomorphism. In other words,

(\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H,q})_{*}\colon K_{*}(\mathcal{A}_{H}^{(q)})\longrightarrow K_{*}(A_{aH}^{(q)})

is an isomorphism. Induction gives the claim for
q=r
.

At
a=0
, we have
A_{0H}\cong C^{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})\cong C(\mathbb{T}^{r})
.
Thus
\mu_{H}=(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{H})_{*}\circ\bigl((\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{H})_{*}\bigr)^{-1}

transports the standard identification
K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{r}))\cong\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{r}

to
K_{*}(A_{H})
. This is the usual deformation/Pimsner–Voiculescu
identification of the
K
-theory of a noncommutative torus.

Now assume
H=L^{t}GL
. We verify that
\Phi_{L}
is well-defined. In

\mathcal{A}_{G}
, one has
\mathcal{V}^{Le_{\ell}}\mathcal{V}^{Le_{j}}=\exp\bigl(2\pi i\,s\langle Le_{j},GLe_{\ell}\rangle\bigr)\mathcal{V}^{Le_{j}}\mathcal{V}^{Le_{\ell}}.

Since
\langle Le_{j},GLe_{\ell}\rangle=(L^{t}GL)_{j,\ell}=H_{j,\ell},

the unitaries
\mathcal{V}^{Le_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{V}^{Le_{r}}
satisfy exactly the
defining relations of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
. Thus the universal property gives the

C([0,1])
-linear
*
-homomorphism
\Phi_{L}\colon\mathcal{A}_{H}\to\mathcal{A}_{G}.

For each
a\in[0,1]
, evaluation gives a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\mathcal{A}_{H}@>{\Phi_{L}}>{}>\mathcal{A}_{G}\\
@V{\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{H}}V{}V@V{}V{\mathrm{ev}_{a}^{G}}V\\
A_{aH}@>{\varphi_{L,1}^{(a)}}>{}>A_{aG},\end{CD}

where
\varphi_{L,1}^{(a)}
is the monomial homomorphism determined by
L
at
parameter
a
. For
a=0
, this is exactly the commutative pullback
f_{L}^{*}\colon C(\mathbb{T}^{r})\to C(\mathbb{T}^{N}).

For
a=1
, it is the noncommutative toric homomorphism

\varphi_{L,1}\colon A_{H}\to A_{G}.

Therefore
(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{G})_{*}\circ(\Phi_{L})_{*}=(\varphi_{L,1})_{*}\circ(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{H})_{*}

and
(\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{G})_{*}\circ(\Phi_{L})_{*}=(f_{L}^{*})_{*}\circ(\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{H})_{*}.

Since the evaluation maps induce
K
-theory isomorphisms, we obtain

\displaystyle(\varphi_{L,1})_{*}\circ\mu_{H}

\displaystyle=(\varphi_{L,1})_{*}\circ(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{H})_{*}\circ\bigl((\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{H})_{*}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{G})_{*}\circ(\Phi_{L})_{*}\circ\bigl((\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{H})_{*}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}_{1}^{G})_{*}\circ\bigl((\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{G})_{*}\bigr)^{-1}\circ(f_{L}^{*})_{*}

\displaystyle=\mu_{G}\circ(f_{L}^{*})_{*}.

This proves the naturality identity. ∎

Conventions 2.6
.

Let
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}

be as in Proposition
2.4
, and assume
\Theta\equiv M^{t}\Psi M\pmod{M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}}.

Put
H:=M^{t}\Psi M.
Since
\Theta-H
has integer entries, the defining commutation constants for

A_{\Theta}
and
A_{H}
are the same. Hence there is a canonical

*
-isomorphism
\chi_{\Theta,H}\colon A_{\Theta}\longrightarrow A_{H}
satisfying

\chi_{\Theta,H}(U_{j}^{\Theta})=U_{j}^{H},
for every
1\leq j\leq m.

For the source algebra
A_{\Theta}
, we use the exterior-algebra identification
obtained by first applying
\chi_{\Theta,H}
and then using the
deformation–Pimsner–Voiculescu identification
\mu_{H}
from
Lemma
2.5
. Equivalently, we identify

\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m}
with
K_{*}(A_{\Theta})
by the map

\nu_{\Theta,M,\Psi}:=(\chi_{\Theta,H})_{*}^{-1}\circ\mu_{H}\colon\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\cong K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{m}))\longrightarrow K_{*}(A_{\Theta}).

For the target algebra
A_{\Psi}
, we use the usual identification

\mu_{\Psi}\colon\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cong K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{n}))\longrightarrow K_{*}(A_{\Psi}).

Thus, when we say that
(\varphi_{M,z})_{*}
is
\Lambda^{*}(M)
, we mean that
\mu_{\Psi}^{-1}\circ(\varphi_{M,z})_{*}\circ\nu_{\Theta,M,\Psi}=\Lambda^{*}(M).

Proposition 2.7

(
K
-theory of monomial maps)
.

Let
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}

be as in Proposition
2.4
. Under the exterior-algebra
identifications described in
Convention
2.6
, the induced maps are
(\varphi_{M,z})_{*0}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)\colon\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{n},

and
(\varphi_{M,z})_{*1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M)\colon\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Equivalently,
\mu_{\Psi}^{-1}\circ(\varphi_{M,z})_{*}\circ\nu_{\Theta,M,\Psi}=\Lambda^{*}(M)

as maps
\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Proof.

We first remove the phase vector
z
. Let
\beta_{z}\in\operatorname{Aut}(A_{\Theta})

be the gauge automorphism defined by
\beta_{z}(U_{j})=z_{j}U_{j},
for every
1\leq j\leq m.

Since
\mathbb{T}^{m}
is path connected,
\beta_{z}
is homotopic to the identity
automorphism. Hence
(\beta_{z})_{*}=\mathrm{Id}

on
K
-theory. Moreover,
\varphi_{M,z}=\varphi_{M,1}\circ\beta_{z}.

Thus it suffices to compute the induced map for
\varphi_{M,1}
.

Put
H:=M^{t}\Psi M.
By Convention
2.6
, we identify
A_{\Theta}
with

A_{H}
by the canonical isomorphism
\chi_{\Theta,H}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{H},

\chi_{\Theta,H}(U_{j}^{\Theta})=U_{j}^{H}.
Under this identification,
\varphi_{M,1}
becomes the toric homomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1}\colon A_{H}\to A_{\Psi},

\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1}(U_{j}^{H})=V^{Me_{j}},
associated to the exact identity
H=M^{t}\Psi M.
That is,
\varphi_{M,1}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1}\circ\chi_{\Theta,H}.

We now apply Lemma
2.5
with
L=M
,
G=\Psi,
and
H=M^{t}\Psi M
. The lemma gives the commutative
K
-theory diagram

\begin{CD}K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{m}))@>{(f_{M}^{*})_{*}}>{}>K_{*}(C(\mathbb{T}^{n}))\\
@V{\mu_{H}}V{}V@V{}V{\mu_{\Psi}}V\\
K_{*}(A_{H})@>{(\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1})_{*}}>{}>K_{*}(A_{\Psi}),\end{CD}

where
f_{M}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{m}

is given by

f_{M}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}^{M_{ij}}\right)_{j=1}^{m}.

Equivalently,
(\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1})_{*}\circ\mu_{H}=\mu_{\Psi}\circ(f_{M}^{*})_{*}.

Since
\nu_{\Theta,M,\Psi}=(\chi_{\Theta,H})_{*}^{-1}\circ\mu_{H}

and
\varphi_{M,1}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1}\circ\chi_{\Theta,H},

we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\Psi}^{-1}\circ(\varphi_{M,1})_{*}\circ\nu_{\Theta,M,\Psi}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Psi}^{-1}\circ(\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1})_{*}\circ(\chi_{\Theta,H})_{*}\circ(\chi_{\Theta,H})_{*}^{-1}\circ\mu_{H}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Psi}^{-1}\circ(\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,1})_{*}\circ\mu_{H}

\displaystyle=(f_{M}^{*})_{*}.

Thus, in the chosen exterior-algebra coordinates, the induced map is exactly

(f_{M}^{*})_{*}
.

It remains to compute
(f_{M}^{*})_{*}
on ordinary torus
K
-theory. Let

u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
be the coordinate functions on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
, and let

v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
be the coordinate functions on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Then
f_{M}^{*}(u_{j})=v_{1}^{M_{1j}}\cdots v_{n}^{M_{nj}}.

Let also
e_{j}=[u_{j}]\in K^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
and
f_{i}=[v_{i}]\in K^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n}).

Under the standard identification
K^{*}(\mathbb{T}^{m})\cong\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m},

the classes
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
are the degree-one exterior generators. Similarly,

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are the degree-one generators of
K^{*}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\cong\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.
Then

(f_{M}^{*})_{*}(e_{j})=[f_{M}^{*}(u_{j})]=[v_{1}^{M_{1j}}\cdots v_{n}^{M_{nj}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{ij}[v_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{ij}f_{i}.

Thus the degree-one part of
(f_{M}^{*})_{*}
is precisely the homomorphism
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Since
f_{M}^{*}
is a graded ring homomorphism and
K^{*}(\mathbb{T}^{m})\cong\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m}

is the exterior algebra on the degree-one generators, the induced map on all
of
K^{*}(\mathbb{T}^{m})
is
\Lambda^{*}(M)\colon\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\Lambda^{*}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Consequently,

(\varphi_{M,1})_{*0}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)
and

(\varphi_{M,1})_{*1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M)

in the chosen exterior-algebra coordinates. Since
(\beta_{z})_{*}=\mathrm{Id}
, the same formulas hold for
\varphi_{M,z}
.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

Mathai and Rosenberg prove an existence criterion for unital

*
-homomorphisms between irrational rotation algebras

[
30
, Theorem 2.1]
. Proposition
2.4

recovers the explicit monomial construction appearing in the sufficiency
direction of their theorem.

Let
t\in(0,1)
, and write
J(t):=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\
-t&0\end{pmatrix}.

For every
M\in M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, one has
M^{t}J(\theta)M=\det(M)\,J(\theta).

Hence, if
\Theta=c\theta+d
, with

c,d\in\mathbb{Z}
,
c\neq 0
,
and if
M\in M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
satisfies
\det(M)=c
, then
J(\Theta)\equiv M^{t}J(\theta)M\pmod{M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}}.

Therefore Proposition
2.4
gives a unital toric

*
-homomorphism
A_{J(\Theta)}\longrightarrow A_{J(\theta)}.

In particular, taking
M=\begin{pmatrix}c&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

gives the monomial map

U_{1}\longmapsto V_{1}^{c},\qquad U_{2}\longmapsto V_{2}.

Equivalently, after translating between the generator convention used here and
the convention
UV=e^{2\pi i\theta}VU
used in

[
30
]
, this is the Mathai–Rosenberg map

U\longmapsto u^{c},\qquad V\longmapsto v.

Conversely, the necessity of the condition
\Theta\in\mathbb{Z}+\theta\mathbb{Z}
for an
arbitrary unital
*
-homomorphism follows from
Theorem
2.2
; equivalently, it follows from the
trace-range description

\rho_{\Theta}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta}))=\mathbb{Z}+\Theta\mathbb{Z},\qquad\rho_{\theta}(K_{0}(A_{\theta}))=\mathbb{Z}+\theta\mathbb{Z},\qquad\rho_{\Theta}([1])=\rho_{\theta}([1])=1.

Indeed, if
A_{\Theta}\to A_{\theta}
is unital, trace compatibility gives
\rho_{\Theta}=\rho_{\theta}\circ\phi_{*0}.

Hence
\rho_{\Theta}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta}))\subseteq\rho_{\theta}(K_{0}(A_{\theta})),

and in particular
\Theta\in\mathbb{Z}+\theta\mathbb{Z}
. Thus
Proposition
2.4
, together with
Theorem
2.2
, recovers

[
30
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Finally, if
|c|=1
, then
M\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, and the associated toric map is
an isomorphism. If
|c|\neq 1
, then Proposition
2.7

shows that the induced map on
K_{1}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

is
M
, which is not an isomorphism. Since a unital homomorphism from the
simple algebra
A_{\Theta}
is injective, surjectivity would force it to be an
isomorphism. Therefore the toric map cannot be onto
A_{\theta}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
A_{\Theta}
and
A_{\Psi}
be simple noncommutative tori of dimensions

m
and
n
, respectively. If there is a nonzero projection

p\in A_{\Psi}
such that
pA_{\Psi}p\cong A_{\Theta},

then
m=n
.

Proof.

Since
A_{\Psi}
is simple and
p\neq 0
, the projection
p
is full. Hence

pA_{\Psi}p
is a full corner of
A_{\Psi}
, and so
pA_{\Psi}p
is strongly
Morita equivalent to
A_{\Psi}
. In particular,
K_{i}(pA_{\Psi}p)\cong K_{i}(A_{\Psi})
,
for
i=0,1
.

If
pA_{\Psi}p\cong A_{\Theta}
, then
K_{i}(A_{\Theta})\cong K_{i}(A_{\Psi})
, for
i=0,1
, as abelian groups. By (
3
),

\mathrm{rank}K_{0}(A_{\Theta})=\mathrm{rank}K_{1}(A_{\Theta})=2^{m-1},

and similarly
\mathrm{rank}K_{0}(A_{\Psi})=\mathrm{rank}K_{1}(A_{\Psi})=2^{n-1}.

Thus
m=n
.
∎

In the proposition below, we use the notation and results from
[
35
,
27
]
.

Proposition 2.10

(Case (2): corner models via strong Morita equivalence)
.

Let
d\geq 2
, and let
\Psi\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric. Let

g=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SO}(d,d\mid\mathbb{Z}),\qquad A,B,C,D\in M_{d}(\mathbb{Z}),

and assume that
C\Psi+D
is invertible. Put
\Psi^{\prime}:=g\cdot\Psi:=(A\Psi+B)(C\Psi+D)^{-1}.

Then
A_{\Psi}
and
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
are strongly Morita equivalent. Consequently,
there exist
k\geq 1
and a full projection
p\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

such that
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\cong pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.
Thus, once such a corner identification is fixed, every monomial map

\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\longrightarrow A_{\Psi^{\prime}}

produces a
*
-homomorphism

A_{\Theta}\longrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

by composition with the corner identification and the inclusion
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\hookrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi}).

If
p\neq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, then this composite is nonunital.

Proof.

The strong Morita equivalence statement is
[
27
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Thus
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
and
A_{\Psi}
are strongly Morita equivalent.

Let
E
be an
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
-
A_{\Psi}
imprimitivity bimodule. Since

A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
is unital, the algebra of compact adjointable operators

\mathcal{K}_{A_{\Psi}}(E)
is unital. Hence
E
is finitely generated
projective as a right Hilbert
A_{\Psi}
-module. Therefore there are

k\geq 1
and a projection
p\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

such that
E\cong pA_{\Psi}^{k}

as right Hilbert
A_{\Psi}
-modules. It follows that

A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{A_{\Psi}}(E)\cong\mathcal{K}_{A_{\Psi}}(pA_{\Psi}^{k})\cong pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Because
E
is an imprimitivity bimodule, it is full as a right

A_{\Psi}
-module; equivalently, the projection
p
is full in

M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.

Now let
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi^{\prime}}

be a monomial map. Choosing an isomorphism
\chi\colon A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\cong}pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

as above, and composing with the corner inclusion, gives a

*
-homomorphism

A_{\Theta}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi_{M,z}\ }A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\ \chi\ }pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\hookrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi}).

Since
\varphi_{M,z}
and
\chi
are unital onto their codomains, this
composite sends
1_{A_{\Theta}}\mapsto p.

Therefore, if
p\neq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, the composite is nonunital as a map
into
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

For
d=2
, the
\mathrm{SO}(2,2\mid\mathbb{Z})
-orbit picture on skew
2\times 2

matrices reduces, at the level of the scalar rotation parameter, to the usual
fractional-linear action

\theta\longmapsto\frac{a\theta+b}{c\theta+d}

of
\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. This is the classical Morita-equivalence picture for
irrational rotation algebras and is closely related to the nonunital part of
the Mathai–Rosenberg classification
[
30
, Theorem 2.7]
.

For
d\geq 3
, being in the same
\mathrm{SO}(d,d\mid\mathbb{Z})
-orbit is a sufficient
condition for strong Morita equivalence of
C^{*}
-algebraic noncommutative
tori, but it is not the most general
C^{*}
-algebraic criterion. The latter is
given in terms of ordered
K_{0}
-groups and centers; see

[
16
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 2.12

(Case (3): embeddings with dimension change)
.

Let
m,n\geq 2
, and let

\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric matrices.
Assume that
\Theta
is nondegenerate. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

There exist
k\geq 1
, a full projection

p\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, a skew-symmetric matrix
\Psi^{\prime}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, an isomorphism
\alpha\colon A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\cong}pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p,

an integer matrix
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
, and a phase vector

z\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
, such that the composite

\phi\colon A_{\Theta}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi_{M,z}\ }A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\ \alpha\ }pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\xrightarrow{\ \iota_{p}\ }M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

is an injective
*
-homomorphism.

(ii)

There exist a skew-symmetric matrix
\Psi^{\prime}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, an integer
matrix
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\operatorname{rank}(M)=m
, and an
integer skew-symmetric matrix
K\in M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}
, such that

A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
is strongly Morita equivalent to
A_{\Psi}
and
\Theta=M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M+K.

In this situation one necessarily has
m\leq n
. Moreover, if
\beta_{i}:=(\iota_{p})_{*i}\circ\alpha_{*i}\colon K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}})\longrightarrow K_{i}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi})),
for
i=0,1,

then, under the exterior-algebra identifications used in
Proposition
2.7
,
\phi_{*0}=\beta_{0}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)
and
\phi_{*1}=\beta_{1}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M).
If, in addition,
\Psi
is nondegenerate, then, under the standard Morita
identification
K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))\cong K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, one has
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\phi_{*0}=\rho_{\Psi}\bigl(\phi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])\bigr)\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Equivalently,
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}\circ\phi=t\,\tau_{\Theta},

where
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{k}\otimes\tau_{\Psi}

is the unnormalized matrix trace and
t=\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)=\rho_{\Psi}\bigl(\phi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])\bigr)>0.

Whenever
p\neq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, the resulting map is genuinely nonunital.

Proof.

Assume (ii). Since
\Theta=M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M+K

with
K\in M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}
, we have
\Theta\equiv M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M\pmod{M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}}.

Hence Proposition
2.4
yields, for every

z\in\mathbb{T}^{m}
, a unital monomial
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi^{\prime}}.

Because
\Theta
is nondegenerate,
A_{\Theta}
is simple, and the unital map
\varphi_{M,z}
is nonzero; hence
\varphi_{M,z}
is injective.

Next, we proceed as in the proof of Proposition
2.10
. Since
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
and
A_{\Psi}
are strongly Morita equivalent, there is an

A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
-
A_{\Psi}
imprimitivity bimodule
E
. Because
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
is
unital,
E
is finitely generated projective as a right Hilbert

A_{\Psi}
-module. Hence there exist
k\geq 1
and a projection
p\in M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

such that
E\cong pA_{\Psi}^{k}.

It follows that
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{K}_{A_{\Psi}}(E)\cong pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Moreover,
p
is full because
E
is an imprimitivity bimodule.

Fix such an
isomorphism
\alpha\colon A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\cong}pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p,

and let
\iota_{p}\colon pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\hookrightarrow M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

be the corner inclusion. Then
\phi:=\iota_{p}\circ\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}

has the required form. Since
\varphi_{M,z}
,
\alpha
, and
\iota_{p}

are injective,
\phi
is injective. This proves (i).

We next compute the induced maps on
K
-theory. By
Proposition
2.7
,
(\varphi_{M,z})_{*0}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)
and

(\varphi_{M,z})_{*1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M).

By functoriality,
\phi_{*i}=(\iota_{p})_{*i}\circ\alpha_{*i}\circ(\varphi_{M,z})_{*i},

for
i=0,1
.
Thus, with
\beta_{i}:=(\iota_{p})_{*i}\circ\alpha_{*i},

we obtain
\phi_{*0}=\beta_{0}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M)
and

\phi_{*1}=\beta_{1}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M).

Assume now, in addition, that
\Psi
is nondegenerate. Then
A_{\Psi}
is
simple and has unique tracial state
\tau_{\Psi}
. We write
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{k}\otimes\tau_{\Psi}

for the unnormalized trace on
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
. Thus the unique tracial state on

M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
is
k^{-1}\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}
. The corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
has unique tracial state

\tau_{p}(x)=\frac{\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(x)}{\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)},\qquad x\in pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

The map
\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

is unital, and both domain and codomain have unique tracial states. Hence
\tau_{p}\circ\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\tau_{\Theta}.

Multiplying by
t:=\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)>0

gives
\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}\circ\phi=t\,\tau_{\Theta}.

Passing to
K_{0}
, and using the standard identification

K_{0}(M_{k}(A_{\Psi}))\cong K_{0}(A_{\Psi})
, gives
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\phi_{*0}=t\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Since
\phi(1_{A_{\Theta}})=p,

we also have
t=\tau_{\Psi}^{(k)}(p)=\rho_{\Psi}\bigl(\phi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])\bigr).

Therefore
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\phi_{*0}=\rho_{\Psi}\bigl(\phi_{*0}([1_{A_{\Theta}}])\bigr)\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Finally, since
\varphi_{M,z}
is unital and
\alpha
is unital onto the
corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
, we have
\phi(1_{A_{\Theta}})=p.

Thus, if
p\neq 1_{M_{k}(A_{\Psi})}
, the map
\phi
is nonunital as a map
into
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
.

Conversely, assume (i). Since
\alpha
is an isomorphism onto a full
corner of
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, the algebra
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
is strongly Morita
equivalent to
M_{k}(A_{\Psi})
, hence to
A_{\Psi}
. Moreover,
Proposition
2.4
, applied to the toric part
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi^{\prime}},

shows that
\Theta\equiv M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M\pmod{M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}}.

Hence there exists
K\in M_{m}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}

such that
\Theta=M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M+K.

It remains to prove that
\operatorname{rank}(M)=m
. Suppose, to the
contrary, that
\operatorname{rank}(M)<m
. Since
M
has integer entries,
there exists a nonzero vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}

such that
Mx=0.

Then, for every
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,
x^{t}\Theta y=x^{t}(M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M)y+x^{t}Ky=(Mx)^{t}\Psi^{\prime}My+x^{t}Ky=x^{t}Ky.

Since
K
has integer entries,
x^{t}Ky\in\mathbb{Z}

for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

Therefore
\exp(2\pi i\,x^{t}\Theta y)=1

for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

This contradicts the nondegeneracy of
\Theta
. Hence
\ker(M)\cap\mathbb{Z}^{m}=\{0\},

and, since
M
is an integer matrix, this forces
\operatorname{rank}(M)=m.

In particular
m\leq n
. This proves (ii) and completes the proof.
∎

In the special case
m=n=d
, Theorem
2.12
reduces to the following result, which provides a more explicit formulation in this setting:

Corollary 2.13

(Case (4) as the same-dimensional noncorner subcase of Case (3))
.

Let
d\geq 2
, and let
\Theta,\Psi\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric matrices.
Assume that
\Theta
is nondegenerate. Then condition
(i)
of
Theorem
2.12
holds if and only if there exist a
skew-symmetric matrix
\Psi^{\prime}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
, an integer matrix

M\in M_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\det(M)\neq 0,

and an integer skew-symmetric matrix

K\in M_{d}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}

such that
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
is strongly Morita equivalent to
A_{\Psi}
and
\Theta=M^{t}\Psi^{\prime}M+K.

For any factorization

\phi=\iota_{p}\circ\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\xrightarrow{\ \varphi_{M,z}\ }A_{\Psi^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\ \alpha\ }pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p\xrightarrow{\ \iota_{p}\ }M_{k}(A_{\Psi})

arising from such data, the range of
\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

is a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of the corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
with unit
p
.
Moreover,
\alpha\bigl(\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})\bigr)\subsetneq pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p

if and only if
|\det(M)|\neq 1.

Equivalently, for this factorization, the range is the whole corner exactly
when
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The first assertion is exactly the special case
m=n=d
of
Theorem
2.12
.
Now fix such a factorization
\phi=\iota_{p}\circ\alpha\circ\varphi_{M,z}.

Since
\varphi_{M,z}
is unital and
\alpha
is a unital isomorphism from

A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
onto the corner
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
, the algebra
\alpha\bigl(\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})\bigr)

is a unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p
with unit
p
.

We first prove that the range is proper when
|\det(M)|\neq 1
. Suppose, to the
contrary, that
\alpha\bigl(\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})\bigr)=pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Applying
\alpha^{-1}
gives
\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})=A_{\Psi^{\prime}}.

Thus
\varphi_{M,z}
is surjective. Since
A_{\Theta}
is simple and

\varphi_{M,z}
is unital,
\varphi_{M,z}
is also injective. Hence

\varphi_{M,z}
would be an isomorphism. By Proposition
2.7
, under the exterior-algebra
identifications, the induced map on
K_{1}
is

(\varphi_{M,z})_{*1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M)\colon\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

This map preserves exterior degree, and its exterior degree-one component is
precisely
M\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

If
|\det(M)|\neq 1
, then
M
is not surjective as a homomorphism

\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Therefore
\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M)
is not surjective.
Consequently
(\varphi_{M,z})_{*1}
cannot be an isomorphism, contradicting
the fact that
\varphi_{M,z}
is an isomorphism. Hence
\alpha\bigl(\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})\bigr)\subsetneq pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Conversely, assume that
|\det(M)|=1
. Then
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
. We show that

\varphi_{M,z}
is surjective. Let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}
be the canonical generators
of
A_{\Psi^{\prime}}
. Since
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
, the columns
Me_{1},\ldots,Me_{d}

form a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. The range of
\varphi_{M,z}
contains the unitaries
z_{j}V^{Me_{j}},
for
1\leq j\leq d.

Multiplying by scalars, it therefore contains each
V^{Me_{j}}
. Since the
vectors
Me_{j}
form a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, every standard basis vector
e_{i}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is an
integer linear combination of them. Hence, using products and adjoints of the
unitaries
V^{Me_{j}}
, the range contains a nonzero scalar multiple of each
canonical generator
V_{i}
. Therefore the range contains all
V_{i}
, and so
\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})=A_{\Psi^{\prime}}.

Thus
\alpha\bigl(\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta})\bigr)=pM_{k}(A_{\Psi})p.

Combining the two directions, the range inside the chosen corner is proper
exactly when
|\det(M)|\neq 1
, and it is the whole corner exactly when

M\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

3.
Elliott invariants of noncommutative protori

Consider now inductive systems of
C^{*}
-algebras
B_{1}\xrightarrow{\ \phi_{1}\ }B_{2}\xrightarrow{\ \phi_{2}\ }B_{3}\xrightarrow{\ \phi_{3}\ }\cdots,
with

B_{n}=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n}}),

where each
\Theta_{n}
is nondegenerate, each
r_{n}\geq 1
, and each
\phi_{n}
is nonzero. Let

A_{\mathrm{pro}}=\varinjlim\bigl(B_{n},\,\phi_{n}\bigr)
be the corresponding noncommutative protori. For each
n
, let
\tau_{n}:=\tau_{n}^{\Theta_{n}}=\frac{1}{r_{n}}\mathrm{Tr}_{r_{n}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta_{n}}
be the normalized tracial state on
B_{n}
. Let

\rho_{n}:=(\tau_{n})_{*}\colon K_{0}(B_{n})\to\mathbb{R},\qquad f_{i,n}:=(\phi_{n})_{*i}\colon K_{i}(B_{n})\to K_{i}(B_{n+1}),\quad i=0,1,

and let
\iota_{n}\colon B_{n}\to A_{\mathrm{pro}}
denote the canonical maps. Because of the uniqueness of the trace, for each
n
there is a unique scalar
t_{n}\in(0,1]
such that
\rho_{n+1}\circ f_{0,n}=t_{n}\,\rho_{n}.

We then define
c_{1}:=1
, and
c_{n}:=(t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n-1})^{-1}
for all
n\geq 2
.

Proposition 3.1

(Regularity properties of simple protori)
.

The following hold.

(i)

Each
\phi_{n}
is injective and full, and
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is simple.

(ii)

A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is separable, nuclear, hence amenable, and satisfies the UCT.

(iii)

A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-stable, and has real rank zero and stable rank one .

Proof.

(i) Each
B_{n}=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n}})
is simple because
A_{\Theta_{n}}
is simple. Since
\phi_{n}\neq 0
and the domain of
\phi_{n}
is simple,
\phi_{n}
is injective. Its range is full because the ideal generated by
\phi_{n}(B_{n})
contains the nonzero projection
\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})
, and
B_{n+1}
is simple. It follows from the standard simplicity criterion for inductive limits with injective full connecting maps that
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is simple.

(ii) Each building block
B_{n}
is separable, nuclear, simple, and in the UCT class (see Theorem
1.1
); matrix algebras
preserve these properties. Since countable inductive limits preserve separability,
nuclearity, and membership in the bootstrap/UCT class,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is separable, nuclear,
and satisfies the UCT.

(iii) For
\mathcal{Z}
-stability, note that each
B_{n}
is a simple unital AT algebra,
hence a simple unital AH algebra of finite topological dimension, as AT algebras
are AH algebras with one-dimensional building blocks.
By
[
42
, Corollary 3.1]
, each
B_{n}
is
\mathcal{Z}
-stable.
Since
\mathcal{Z}
is strongly self-absorbing and
\mathcal{Z}
-stability passes to inductive
limits of separable
C^{*}
-algebras by
[
41
, Corollary 3.4]
, it follows that
A_{\mathrm{pro}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}.

Finally, each
B_{n}
has real rank zero, and real rank zero is preserved under
inductive limits
[
2
]
. Hence
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
has real rank zero. Similarly, since each
B_{n}
has stable rank one and stable rank one is preserved under inductive limits,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
has stable rank one as well.
∎

The use of real-rank-zero structure and ordered
K
-theory in the
classification of these limits is in the spirit of Elliott’s real-rank-zero
classification program
[
17
]
. For the simple
C^{*}
-algebras relevant here, we use the unified nonunital
Elliott invariant in the form described in

[
22
, Definition 5.8]
.

Let
A
be a separable simple
C^{*}
-algebra with

\widetilde{T}(A)\neq\{0\}
, where
\widetilde{T}(A)
denotes the cone of
densely defined positive lower semicontinuous traces on
A
, equipped with
the topology of pointwise convergence on the Pedersen ideal
\mathrm{Ped}(A)
.
The scale function is the element
\Sigma_{A}\in\mathrm{LAff}_{+}(\widetilde{T}(A))

defined pointwise by
\Sigma_{A}(\tau):=\sup\{\tau(a):a\in\mathrm{Ped}(A)_{+},\ \|a\|\leq 1\},
for all
\tau\in\widetilde{T}(A).
Equivalently, if
e_{A}\in A_{+}
is a strictly positive element, then

\Sigma_{A}(\tau)=d_{\tau}(e_{A}),
for
\tau\in\widetilde{T}(A)
,
and this function is independent of the choice of
e_{A}
.

The trace pairing is denoted
\rho_{A}\colon K_{0}(A)\to\mathrm{Aff}(\widetilde{T}(A)).

For finite separable amenable simple
\mathcal{Z}
-stable
C^{*}
-algebras,
the positive cone is determined by the trace pairing:
K_{0}(A)^{+}=\{x\in K_{0}(A):\rho_{A}(x)>0\}\cup\{0\},

where
\rho_{A}(x)>0
means that
\rho_{A}(x)
is strictly positive on

\widetilde{T}(A)\setminus\{0\}
.

The distinguished set
\Sigma_{\rho}(K_{0}(A))
is defined from the scale function
as follows. In the unital case,

\Sigma_{\rho}(K_{0}(A))=\{x\in K_{0}(A)^{+}:\rho_{A}(x)<\Sigma_{A}\}\cup\{[1_{A}]\}.

In the nonunital case,

\Sigma_{\rho}(K_{0}(A))=\{x\in K_{0}(A)^{+}:\rho_{A}(x)<\Sigma_{A}\}.

Here the inequality
\rho_{A}(x)<\Sigma_{A}
is understood in the strict order used for scaled
ordered group pairings:
\Sigma_{A}-\rho_{A}(x)\in\mathrm{Aff}_{+}(\widetilde{T}(A))
and
\Sigma_{A}-\rho_{A}(x)\neq 0
.
Equivalently,
\rho_{A}(x)(\tau)<\Sigma_{A}(\tau)
for all nonzero

\tau\in\widetilde{T}(A)
, in the cases considered here.

The unified Elliott invariant is

\mathrm{Ell}(A):=\bigl((K_{0}(A),\Sigma_{\rho}(K_{0}(A)),\widetilde{T}(A),\Sigma_{A},\rho_{A}),K_{1}(A)\bigr).

For finite separable amenable simple
\mathcal{Z}
-stable algebras,

[
22
, Theorem 5.11]
gives
\Sigma_{\rho}(K_{0}(A))=\Sigma(K_{0}(A)),

where
\Sigma(K_{0}(A)):=\{[p]\in K_{0}(A):p\in A\text{ is a projection}\}

is the projection scale of
A
. Thus, in the present finite setting, one may
equivalently write

\mathrm{Ell}(A)=\bigl((K_{0}(A),\Sigma(K_{0}(A)),\widetilde{T}(A),\Sigma_{A},\rho_{A}),K_{1}(A)\bigr).

If
A
is unital, then
\Sigma(K_{0}(A))
has maximum element
[1_{A}]
, and
the invariant can be written in the familiar unital form

\mathrm{Ell}(A)=\bigl(K_{0}(A),K_{0}(A)^{+},[1_{A}],T(A),\rho_{A},K_{1}(A)\bigr).

For nonunital finite separable amenable simple
\mathcal{Z}
-stable

C^{*}
-algebras,
[
22
, Theorem 5.13]
shows that the
projection scale is determined by the scale function. Hence, in the nonunital
case, the invariant may be written in the reduced form

(10)

\mathrm{Ell}(A)=\bigl((K_{0}(A),\widetilde{T}(A),\Sigma_{A},\rho_{A}),K_{1}(A)\bigr).

If
A
is stable, then the scale function carries no additional information,
and the invariant reduces further to

\mathrm{Ell}(A)=\bigl(K_{0}(A),\widetilde{T}(A),\rho_{A},K_{1}(A)\bigr).

By the isomorphism theorem of Gong and Lin

[
23
, Theorem 14.9]
, equivalently

[
22
, Theorem 5.13]
, the above Elliott invariant is a
complete isomorphism invariant for finite separable amenable simple

\mathcal{Z}
-stable
C^{*}
-algebras satisfying the UCT:

A\cong B\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Ell}(A)\cong\mathrm{Ell}(B).

In the nonunital case, one may equivalently use the reduced invariant
(
10
).

The invariant
\mathrm{Ell}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
of our
C^{*}
-algebra
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is described below.

Theorem 3.2

(Elliott invariant of a protoral system)
.

Let
A_{\mathrm{pro}}=\varinjlim(B_{n},\phi_{n})

be as above.

(i)

For
i=0,1
, one has
K_{i}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\cong\varinjlim(K_{i}(B_{n}),f_{i,n}).
The positive cone on
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
is the direct-limit cone.

(ii)

The cone
\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
of densely defined lower semicontinuous
traces on
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is one-dimensional. More precisely, there is a densely
defined lower semicontinuous trace
\tau
on
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
, unique up to
multiplication by a positive scalar, such that
\tau\circ\iota_{n}=c_{n}\tau_{n}

for all
n\geq 1
.
Equivalently, if
\rho_{\tau}:=\tau_{*}\colon K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\to\mathbb{R}

then
\rho_{\tau}\circ(\iota_{n})_{*}=c_{n}\rho_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.
The full trace pairing
\rho_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}\colon K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\to\mathrm{Aff}(\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))

is given by
\rho_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(x)(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\,\rho_{\tau}(x)
for all

x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
and
\lambda\in[0,\infty).

(iii)

The scale function is
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\,\sup_{n\geq 1}c_{n},
for every
\lambda\in[0,\infty),

where the value
+\infty
is allowed, with the convention
0\cdot\infty=0
.

(iv)

The projection scale is
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[0,(\iota_{n})_{*}([1_{B_{n}}])]\subseteq K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}.

(v)

If every
\phi_{n}
is unital, then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is unital,

t_{n}=1
for all
n
, and
\tau
is the unique tracial state on

A_{\mathrm{pro}}
.

(vi)

If
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is nonunital and
c_{n}\to\infty
, then
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}.

Proof.

Set
e_{n}:=\iota_{n}(1_{B_{n}})\in A_{\mathrm{pro}}.
Then
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an increasing approximate unit of projections
for
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
.

(i)
Continuity of
K
-theory for inductive limits gives
K_{i}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\cong\varinjlim(K_{i}(B_{n}),f_{i,n})
for

i=0,1.
It remains to identify the positive cone. Let
V(A)
denote the
Murray–von Neumann semigroup of projections in matrix algebras over a

C^{*}
-algebra
A
. The functor
V
is continuous for inductive limits, so
V(A_{\mathrm{pro}})\cong\varinjlim V(B_{n}).

Each
B_{n}
has real rank zero and stable rank one. Hence the natural map
V(B_{n})\longrightarrow K_{0}(B_{n})^{+}

is an isomorphism of ordered semigroups. Passing to the direct limit gives that

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}
is precisely the direct-limit cone.

(ii)
First we construct the trace. Since
\tau_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=t_{n}\tau_{n},
the traces
c_{n}\tau_{n}

are compatible with the connecting maps:
c_{n+1}\tau_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=c_{n+1}t_{n}\tau_{n}=c_{n}\tau_{n}.

Thus they define a positive trace
\tau_{0}
on the algebraic inductive limit
\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\iota_{n}(B_{n}),
defined by

\tau_{0}(\iota_{n}(a)):=c_{n}\tau_{n}(a),
if

a\in B_{n}.

Let
\tau
be its lower semicontinuous regularization on the
C^{*}
-completion

A_{\mathrm{pro}}
:

\tau(x):=\sup\{\tau_{0}(b):\,b\in(\bigcup_{n}\iota_{n}(B_{n}))_{+},\ b\leq x\}.

Then
\tau
is a densely defined lower semicontinuous trace on
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
,
and, since each
c_{n}\tau_{n}
is bounded on
B_{n}
, the regularization agrees
with
c_{n}\tau_{n}
on the finite stages, i.e.
\tau\circ\iota_{n}=c_{n}\tau_{n}
for all

n\geq 1
.
Indeed, if
x\in\iota_{n}(B_{n})_{+}
and
b\in\bigcup_{m}\iota_{m}(B_{m})_{+}
satisfies

b\leq x
, then, after passing to a common later stage, the compatibility of
the traces gives
\tau_{0}(b)\leq\tau_{0}(x)
. Hence the regularization takes the
value
\tau_{0}(x)=c_{n}\tau_{n}(x)
on
x
.

Passing to
K_{0}
gives
\rho_{\tau}\circ(\iota_{n})_{*}=c_{n}\rho_{n},

where
\rho_{\tau}=\tau_{*}
. This proves the displayed formula.

We now prove uniqueness of the trace ray. For
m\geq n
, write
\phi_{m,n}:=\phi_{m-1}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{n}
and

\phi_{n,n}:=\mathrm{Id}_{B_{n}}.
Inside
B_{m}
, put
p_{n,m}:=\phi_{m,n}(1_{B_{n}})
, and set
C_{n,m}:=p_{n,m}B_{m}p_{n,m}
for simplicity.
Then
C_{n,m}
is a nonzero full corner of the simple algebra
B_{m}
. Hence

C_{n,m}
is simple and has a unique tracial state, namely

\omega_{n,m}(x)=\frac{\tau_{m}(x)}{\tau_{m}(p_{n,m})},\qquad x\in C_{n,m}.

The maps
C_{n,m}\longrightarrow C_{n,m+1}

induced by
\phi_{m}
are unital. Moreover, the traces
\omega_{n,m}
are
compatible, because for
x\in C_{n,m}
,

\frac{\tau_{m+1}(\phi_{m}(x))}{\tau_{m+1}(\phi_{m}(p_{n,m}))}=\frac{t_{m}\tau_{m}(x)}{t_{m}\tau_{m}(p_{n,m})}=\omega_{n,m}(x).

Therefore
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}\cong\varinjlim_{m\geq n}(C_{n,m},\phi_{m}|_{C_{n,m}})

is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra with a unique tracial state. Denote this state by
\omega_{n}.

Let
\sigma\in\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
be a nonzero densely defined lower
semicontinuous trace. Since
\sigma
is densely defined, it is finite on the
Pedersen ideal of
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
. In particular, it is finite on projections, so
\lambda_{n}:=\sigma(e_{n})<\infty
for every
n\geq 1
.
The restriction of
\sigma
to the unital algebra
\iota_{n}(B_{n})
, whose unit
is
e_{n}
, is therefore a bounded trace. Since
B_{n}
has a unique tracial
state, there is a scalar
\lambda_{n}\geq 0
such that
\sigma\circ\iota_{n}=\lambda_{n}\tau_{n}.

Compatibility with the connecting maps gives

\lambda_{n}\tau_{n}=\sigma\circ\iota_{n}=\sigma\circ\iota_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=\lambda_{n+1}\tau_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=\lambda_{n+1}t_{n}\tau_{n}.

Thus
\lambda_{n+1}={\lambda_{n}}{t_{n}}^{-1},
and hence

\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{1}c_{n},

for all
n\geq 1
.

If
\lambda_{1}=0
, then
\lambda_{n}=0
for every
n
. Since
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}

has unique tracial state, this implies
\sigma|_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}=0

for all
n\geq 1
. For
a\in(A_{\mathrm{pro}})_{+}
, the positive elements
a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2}

increase to
a
and converge to

a
in norm. By traciality,

\sigma(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2})=\sigma(e_{n}ae_{n})=0.

Lower semicontinuous traces satisfy monotone convergence, so
\sigma(a)=\sup_{n}\sigma(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2})=0.

Thus
\sigma=0
, contrary to our assumption. Hence
\lambda_{1}>0
.

Since
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
has unique tracial state and
\sigma(e_{n})=\lambda_{n}
, we have
\sigma|_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}=\lambda_{n}\omega_{n}=\lambda_{1}c_{n}\omega_{n}.

On the other hand, the trace
\tau
constructed above satisfies
\tau(e_{n})=c_{n},

and hence
\tau|_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}=c_{n}\omega_{n}.

Therefore
\sigma|_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}=\lambda_{1}\,\tau|_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Now let
a\in(A_{\mathrm{pro}})_{+}
. Again
a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2}\nearrow a,

and by traciality,

\sigma(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2})=\sigma(e_{n}ae_{n})=\lambda_{1}\tau(e_{n}ae_{n})=\lambda_{1}\tau(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2}).

Using monotone convergence for both lower semicontinuous traces gives

\sigma(a)=\sup_{n}\sigma(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2})=\lambda_{1}\sup_{n}\tau(a^{1/2}e_{n}a^{1/2})=\lambda_{1}\tau(a).

Thus every nonzero densely defined lower semicontinuous trace is a positive
scalar multiple of
\tau
. Hence
\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})=\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau.
The final displayed formula in (ii) follows from homogeneity:
for
x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})
and
\lambda\geq 0
,
\rho_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(x)(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\,\tau_{*}(x).

(iii)
Let
h\in(A_{\mathrm{pro}})_{+}
be a strictly positive element. By the definition of the scale
function in the unified invariant,
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(\tau)=d_{\tau}(h).

The support projection of
h
in
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}
is
1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}}
. Let

\bar{\tau}
denote the normal extension of
\tau
to
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}
. Then

d_{\tau}(h)=\bar{\tau}(1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}}).

Since
(e_{n})
is an increasing approximate unit of projections,
e_{n}\nearrow 1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}}
strongly. Therefore

\bar{\tau}(1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{**}})=\sup_{n\geq 1}\tau(e_{n})=\sup_{n\geq 1}c_{n}.

Thus
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(\tau)=\sup_{n\geq 1}c_{n}.

By homogeneity of the scale function,
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}}(\lambda\tau)=\lambda\,\sup_{n\geq 1}c_{n},
for
\lambda\in[0,\infty).

(iv)
We prove the projection-scale formula
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[0,[e_{n}]].

Here and below
[e_{n}]
means
(\iota_{n})_{*}([1_{B_{n}}])
.

First let
p\in A_{\mathrm{pro}}
be a projection. Since
e_{n}\to 1
strictly, we have
e_{n}pe_{n}\to p
in norm. For
n

sufficiently large,
e_{n}pe_{n}
is close enough to
p
that the standard
projection perturbation lemma gives a projection
q\in e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
, equivalently

q\leq e_{n}
, which is Murray–von Neumann equivalent to
p
. Hence

[p]=[q]\leq[e_{n}].

Therefore every projection class in
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
belongs to

\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[0,[e_{n}]].

Conversely, suppose
x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}
and

0\leq x\leq[e_{n}]
for some
n
. Since
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is simple and
e_{n}\neq 0
, the corner
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}

is full. Hence the inclusion
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}\hookrightarrow A_{\mathrm{pro}}

induces an order isomorphism on
K_{0}
. Let
x^{\prime}\in K_{0}(e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n})^{+}
be the
preimage of
x
. Then
0\leq x^{\prime}\leq[1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}].

The algebra
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
has real rank zero and stable rank one, being a unital
corner of
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
. Therefore
x^{\prime}
is represented by a projection
in
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
.
Indeed, as
0\leq x^{\prime}\leq[1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}]
, choose projections
q,r
in matrix
algebras over
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
with
[q]=x^{\prime}
and

[r]=[1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}]-x^{\prime}.

Then
[q]+[r]=[1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}].
Since
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
has stable rank one, projections over

e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
satisfy cancellation; equivalently, the natural map
V(e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n})\to K_{0}(e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n})^{+}

is injective. Hence the equality
[q]+[r]=[1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}]

in
K_{0}
implies that
q\oplus r
is Murray–von Neumann equivalent to

1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}
in a matrix algebra. Therefore
q
is Murray–von
Neumann equivalent to a subprojection of
1_{e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}}
, so
x^{\prime}
is
represented by an actual projection in
e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}
.

Therefore there exists a projection
p\in e_{n}A_{\mathrm{pro}}e_{n}\subseteq A_{\mathrm{pro}}

such that

[p]=x.
Thus every element of
[0,[e_{n}]]
is a projection class in
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
. This proves

\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[0,[e_{n}]]=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[0,(\iota_{n})_{*}([1_{B_{n}}])].

(v)
Assume every
\phi_{n}
is unital. Then

\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})=1_{B_{n+1}},

so
e_{n}=e_{n+1}

for all
n
. Hence
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is unital with unit
e_{1}
. Moreover,
