t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}}))=\tau_{n+1}(1_{B_{n+1}})=1.

Thus
c_{n}=1
for all
n
. The trace
\tau
satisfies

\tau(e_{1})=1,

so
\tau
is a tracial state. Since the whole lower semicontinuous trace cone
is
\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau
, this tracial state is unique.

(vi)
Assume
A_{\mathrm{pro}}
is nonunital and
c_{n}\to\infty
. Let

x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}.

If
x=0
, then
x\in\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))
.
Now assume
x\neq 0
. By (i), there exist
n
and
y\in K_{0}(B_{n})^{+}\setminus\{0\}
such
that
(\iota_{n})_{*}(y)=x.

Let
m\geq n
, and write

f_{0,m,n}:=f_{0,m-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0,n}

for the induced map from
K_{0}(B_{n})
to
K_{0}(B_{m})
. Put

y_{m}:=f_{0,m,n}(y)\in K_{0}(B_{m})^{+}.
But

\rho_{\tau}(x)=c_{m}\rho_{m}(y_{m})
by (ii)
so
\rho_{m}(y_{m})=c_{m}^{-1}\rho_{\tau}(x).

Since
c_{m}\to\infty
, choose
m\geq n
so large that

c_{m}>\rho_{\tau}(x).

Then
\rho_{m}(y_{m})<1=\rho_{m}([1_{B_{m}}]).

Hence
\rho_{m}([1_{B_{m}}]-y_{m})>0.
But
the order on
K_{0}(B_{m})
is determined by the unique trace, so

[1_{B_{m}}]-y_{m}\in K_{0}(B_{m})^{+}.

Thus
0\leq y_{m}\leq[1_{B_{m}}].

Applying
(\iota_{m})_{*}
, we get

0\leq x\leq(\iota_{m})_{*}([1_{B_{m}}])=[e_{m}].

By (iv),
x\in\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})).

Therefore
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}})^{+}
, as claimed. The proof is complete.
∎

In the setting of Theorem
3.2
,
B_{n}=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n}})

and
let
\mu_{i,n}\colon K_{i}(B_{n})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(A_{\Theta_{n}})

denote the standard Morita identifications, for
i=0,1
.
The following formulas describe the
induced maps on
K
-theory and the trace-scaling constants.

Proposition 3.3

(The four classes of connecting maps on invariants)
.

(1) [Unital toric maps].

Suppose
r_{n+1}=r_{n}
and
\phi_{n}=\mathrm{Id}_{M_{r_{n}}}\otimes\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}:M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n}})\longrightarrow M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n+1}}),

where
\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}
is the monomial map of
Proposition
2.4
. Then, under the Morita identifications

\mu_{i,n}
and
\mu_{i,n+1}
,
\mu_{0,n+1}\circ f_{0,n}\circ\mu_{0,n}^{-1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})

and
\mu_{1,n+1}\circ f_{1,n}\circ\mu_{1,n}^{-1}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n}).

Moreover,
t_{n}=1.

More generally, if a unital diagonal amplification with
s_{n}
identical
toric blocks is inserted, so that
r_{n+1}=s_{n}r_{n}
, then the right-hand sides
are multiplied by
s_{n}
, while still
t_{n}=1
.

(2) [Pure corner maps].

Suppose
\phi_{n}=\iota_{p_{n}}\circ\alpha_{n}:B_{n}\xrightarrow{\ \alpha_{n}\ }p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}\xrightarrow{\ \iota_{p_{n}}\ }B_{n+1},

where
p_{n}\in B_{n+1}
is a nonzero full projection and

\alpha_{n}
is a unital isomorphism onto the full corner
p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}
.
Then
f_{i,n}=(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}\circ(\alpha_{n})_{*}
for
i=0,1.

The map
(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}
is the
K
-theory isomorphism induced by the
full-corner Morita equivalence. The trace-scaling constant is

t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})).

(3) [Dimension-changing corner maps].

Suppose
\phi_{n}=\iota_{p_{n}}\circ\alpha_{n}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{M_{r_{n}}}\otimes\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}),

where
\mathrm{Id}_{M_{r_{n}}}\otimes\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}:B_{n}\longrightarrow C_{n}:=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})

is induced by a full-column-rank matrix
M_{n}\in M_{d_{n+1}\times d_{n}}(\mathbb{Z}),

\Psi^{\prime}_{n}\in M_{d_{n+1}}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathrm{skew}}
,
and
\alpha_{n}:C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}

is a unital corner identification. Let
\nu_{i,n}\colon K_{i}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})

be the standard Morita identification, and define

\beta_{i,n}:=(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}\circ(\alpha_{n})_{*}\circ\nu_{i,n}^{-1}:K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})\longrightarrow K_{i}(B_{n+1}).

Then
f_{0,n}=\beta_{0,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{0,n},

and
f_{1,n}=\beta_{1,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{1,n}.

Moreover,
t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})).

(4) [Same-dimensional proper toric maps].

Suppose
\phi_{n}=\iota_{p_{n}}\circ\alpha_{n}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{M_{r_{n}}}\otimes\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}),

where now
M_{n}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
is such that

|\det(M_{n})|>1,
and
\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}:A_{\Theta_{n}}\to A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}}

is the corresponding same-dimensional toric map. Let

C_{n}:=M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})
and let
\alpha_{n}:C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}

be a unital corner identification. Define

\beta_{i,n}:=(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}\circ(\alpha_{n})_{*}\circ\nu_{i,n}^{-1}:K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})\longrightarrow K_{i}(B_{n+1}),

where
\nu_{i,n}:K_{i}(C_{n})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}})

is the standard Morita identification. Then
f_{0,n}=\beta_{0,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{0,n},

and
f_{1,n}=\beta_{1,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{1,n}.

Moreover,
t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})).

Proof.

In Case (1), the toric part is unital. By
Proposition
2.7
, with the exterior-algebra convention of
Convention
2.6
, the toric map contributes
\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})

on
K_{0}

and
\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})
on
K_{1}
.
Matrix amplification by
M_{r_{n}}
does not change these maps under the
standard Morita identifications. Hence the displayed formulas hold. Since the
map is unital and both the domain and codomain have unique normalized traces,

\tau_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=\tau_{n}
,
so
t_{n}=1
.

For the diagonal-amplified variant, write
\psi_{n}:=\mathrm{Id}_{M_{r_{n}}}\otimes\varphi_{M_{n},z_{n}}.

The map is

a\longmapsto\operatorname{diag}(\psi_{n}(a),\ldots,\psi_{n}(a))

with
s_{n}
diagonal blocks. On
K
-theory, block diagonal sum adds classes,
so the induced map is
s_{n}
times the map induced by
\psi_{n}
. Thus the
right-hand sides are multiplied by
s_{n}
. On traces, the normalized trace on

M_{s_{n}r_{n}}(A_{\Theta_{n+1}})
contains the factor
1/s_{n}
, which cancels
the
s_{n}
identical blocks. Hence the normalized trace is still preserved, and
again
t_{n}=1
.

In Case (2), the equality
f_{i,n}=(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}\circ(\alpha_{n})_{*}

is simply functoriality of
K
-theory. Since
\alpha_{n}
is unital onto the
corner, for
a\in B_{n}
we have
\phi_{n}(a)\in p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}.

The unique normalized trace on the corner
p_{n}B_{n+1}p_{n}
is
x\longmapsto\frac{\tau_{n+1}(x)}{\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})}.

Thus

\frac{\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(a))}{\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})}=\tau_{n}(a),

because
\alpha_{n}
is a unital isomorphism between uniquely traced unital
simple algebras. Hence
\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(a))=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})\tau_{n}(a),
so
t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})).

Cases (3) and (4) are obtained by the same two ingredients.
The toric part contributes
\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})
on
K_{0}

and
\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})
on
K_{1}
by Proposition
2.7
, again with the convention
of Convention
2.6
. The corner part contributes

(\iota_{p_{n}})_{*}\circ(\alpha_{n})_{*}.

After inserting the Morita identification
\nu_{i,n}:K_{i}(M_{r_{n}}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}}))\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{i}(A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}}),

this gives exactly
f_{0,n}=\beta_{0,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{0,n},

and
f_{1,n}=\beta_{1,n}\circ\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})\circ\mu_{1,n}.

Since the toric part is unital and trace-preserving with respect to normalized
traces, and since
\alpha_{n}
is unital onto the corner, the same corner-trace
calculation as in Case (2) gives
\tau_{n+1}\circ\phi_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})\tau_{n}.

Therefore
t_{n}=\tau_{n+1}(p_{n})=\tau_{n+1}(\phi_{n}(1_{B_{n}})).

In Case (4), the condition
|\det(M_{n})|>1
is precisely the
same-dimensional proper toric subalgebra condition: if
|\det(M_{n})|=1
, then

M_{n}\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Z})
, and the toric part is onto the intermediate torus. If
|\det(M_{n})|>1
, then
M_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is not surjective. Hence

\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})
is not an isomorphism. Since
A_{\Theta_{n}}

is simple, the unital toric map is injective. If it were also surjective, it
would be an isomorphism and would induce an isomorphism on
K_{1}
, a
contradiction. Therefore its range is a proper unital subalgebra of

A_{\Psi^{\prime}_{n}}
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

We now explain the relation with the noncommutative solenoids of
Latrémolière and Packer. Let
N>1
and let
\alpha=(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}\in\Xi_{N}
in the sense of

[
26
, Theorem 2.1]
. For each
n\geq 0
, set

\Theta_{n}:=J(\alpha_{2n})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha_{2n}\\
-\alpha_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem 3.6 of
[
26
]
realizes the noncommutative solenoid

A_{\alpha}^{\mathscr{S}}
as the inductive limit of the rotation algebras

A_{\alpha_{2n}}
under the embeddings
\varphi_{n}\colon A_{\alpha_{2n}}\longrightarrow A_{\alpha_{2n+2}}
,

U_{\alpha_{2n}}\longmapsto U^{N}_{\alpha_{2n+2}},\qquad V_{\alpha_{2n}}\longmapsto V^{N}_{\alpha_{2n+2}}.

These are exactly the unital toric maps of Case (1) corresponding to the
matrix
M=NI_{2}
, with trivial phase vector
z=(1,1)
. Indeed, since

\alpha\in\Xi_{N}
, for each
n
one has
N\alpha_{n+1}-\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{Z},

and iterating once gives
N^{2}\alpha_{2n+2}-\alpha_{2n}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore
M^{t}\Theta_{n+1}M=N^{2}J(\alpha_{2n+2})\equiv J(\alpha_{2n})=\Theta_{n}\pmod{M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{skew}}},

so Proposition
2.4
applies. Thus the noncommutative
solenoids of
[
26
]
form a distinguished subclass of the Case (1)
systems considered in the present paper.

This observation places the irrational solenoids squarely inside our general
direct-limit framework. Indeed,
[
26
, Proposition 3.19]
shows that
if
\alpha_{0}\notin\mathbb{Q}
(equivalently, if some
\alpha_{k}
is irrational), then

A_{\alpha}^{\mathscr{S}}
is a simple AT-algebra of real rank zero. Hence, for
this subclass, the Elliott invariant is complete. Moreover,

[
26
, Theorem 3.7]
computes the corresponding direct-limit invariant
explicitly:
K_{1}(A_{\alpha}^{\mathscr{S}})\cong\mathbb{Z}[1/N]^{2}
and
K_{0}(A_{\alpha}^{\mathscr{S}})\cong K_{\alpha},

where

K_{\alpha}=\left\{\left(z+\frac{pJ_{k}^{\alpha}}{N^{k}},\frac{p}{N^{k}}\right):\ z,p\in\mathbb{Z},\ k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[1/N]^{2},\qquad J_{k}^{\alpha}:=N^{k}\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0},

with distinguished order unit
[1]
and trace map

K_{0}(\tau)\colon\left(z+\frac{pJ_{k}^{\alpha}}{N^{k}},\frac{p}{N^{k}}\right)\longmapsto z+p\alpha_{k}.

From our point of view, these are the invariant data of a Case (1)
system whose toric matrices are all
NI_{2}
. In the exterior-algebra
coordinates used in Proposition
2.7
, the
K
-theory
maps are given by
\Lambda^{*}(NI_{2})
.
Thus the induced map on
K_{1}
is

NI_{2}
, giving
\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},NI_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}[1/N]^{2}.

On
K_{0}
, the degree-zero class is fixed and the top exterior class is
multiplied by
N^{2}
. In the usual rotation-algebra
coordinates used in
[
26
]
, the integer congruence terms

N^{2}\alpha_{2n+2}-\alpha_{2n}
produce the shear terms encoded by

J_{k}^{\alpha}=N^{k}\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{0}
in the group
K_{\alpha}
. Although the inductive-limit model uses the even
subsequence
(\alpha_{2n})
, the subgroup obtained from the even indices is
the same subgroup
K_{\alpha}
written above. Indeed, if
k\geq 0
and
\ell\geq k
is even, then

N^{\ell-k}\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Writing this integer as
s
, one has
J_{\ell}^{\alpha}=N^{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{0}=J_{k}^{\alpha}+N^{k}s.

Thus an element represented using the index
k
can also be represented using
the even index
\ell
, after changing the integer
z
. Hence the even
subsequence gives the same subgroup
K_{\alpha}
.

The full classification theorem
[
26
, Theorem 4.2]
is slightly
broader than the present Elliott-invariant discussion, because it treats all
noncommutative solenoids, including regimes outside the simple AT real-rank-zero
case. For example, the periodic rational algebras described in

[
26
, Theorem 3.20]
are not simple, and the authors note immediately
after that theorem that the embeddings from
[
26
, Theorem 3.6]
then
land in the centers of the rotation algebras. For the irrational subclass,
however,
[
26
, Theorem 4.2]
may be viewed as an explicit translation
of equality of the Elliott invariant into the sequence language of
\Xi_{N}
: the
requirement that
N
and
M
have the same prime factors is already reflected in
the isomorphism type of
K_{1}(A_{\alpha}^{\mathscr{S}})\cong\mathbb{Z}[1/N]^{2},

while the common-subsequence/sign condition records the agreement of the ordered

K_{0}
-extension together with its distinguished order unit and trace. In this
sense,
[
26
, Theorem 4.2]
is the two-dimensional solenoid analogue
of the general principle developed here: once one is in the simple AT
real-rank-zero regime, explicit computations of the direct-limit Elliott
invariant lead to concrete isomorphism criteria.

Example 3.5

(A unital toric
N
-solenoid and its classification)
.

Fix an irrational number
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and an integer
N\geq 2
. For

n\geq 1
, set
\theta_{n}:=\frac{\theta}{N^{2(n-1)}}
and
A_{n}:=A_{\theta_{n}}
,
and let
U_{n},V_{n}
denote the canonical generators of
A_{n}
. Since
N^{2}\theta_{n+1}=\theta_{n},

we have
(NI_{2})^{t}J(\theta_{n+1})(NI_{2})=J(\theta_{n})
, where

J(\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\
-\alpha&0\end{pmatrix}.

Thus Proposition
2.4
gives a unital toric map
\phi_{n}\colon A_{n}\to A_{n+1}

corresponding to the matrix
NI_{2}
, explicitly
\phi_{n}(U_{n})=U_{n+1}^{N},
and

\phi_{n}(V_{n})=V_{n+1}^{N}.

Define

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}:=\varinjlim(A_{n},\phi_{n}).

Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}
is a simple unital noncommutative protorus. Let
R_{N}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/N].

Since
R_{N}=\mathbb{Z}[1/N^{2}]
, Proposition
3.3
gives

f_{0,n}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(NI_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&N^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad f_{1,n}=\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(NI_{2})=NI_{2}.

Therefore

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N})\cong\varinjlim\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&N^{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{N},

and
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N})\cong\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},NI_{2})\cong R_{N}^{2}.

Under the above identification of
K_{0}
, the order unit is
[1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}}]=(1,0).

All connecting maps are unital, so
t_{n}=1
for every
n
. Hence
Theorem
3.2
gives a unique tracial state on

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}
. At stage
n
,
\rho_{n}(K_{0}(A_{n}))=\mathbb{Z}+\theta_{n}\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\theta}{N^{2(n-1)}}\mathbb{Z}.

Passing to the direct limit gives

\rho_{\theta,N}(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{\theta}{N^{2(n-1)}}\mathbb{Z}\right)=\mathbb{Z}+\theta R_{N},

where
\rho_{\theta,N}:=(\tau_{\theta,N})_{*}\colon K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N})\to\mathbb{R}

is the trace map induced by the unique tracial state
\tau_{\theta,N}
.
Under the identification
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{N},

one has
\rho_{\theta,N}(a,q)=a+\theta q.

Consequently

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N})^{+}=\{(a,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{N}:a+\theta q>0\}\cup\{0\},

and
[1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}}]=(1,0).

We now classify these algebras by their Elliott invariants. Let

\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and
M\geq 2
, and construct

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},M}
in the same way. Let
\mathcal{P}(N):=\{p\text{ prime}:p\mid N\}.

Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},M}

as unital
C^{*}
-algebras if and only if
\mathcal{P}(N)=\mathcal{P}(M)

and
\frac{\theta}{\theta^{\prime}}\in R_{N}^{\times}.

Here

R_{N}^{\times}=\left\{\pm\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}(N)}p^{k_{p}}:k_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

If
\theta,\theta^{\prime}>0
, the second condition is equivalently
\theta=u\theta^{\prime}

for some positive unit
u\in R_{N}^{\times}
.

Indeed, suppose first that
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},M}.

Then their
K_{1}
-groups are isomorphic:

R_{N}^{2}\cong R_{M}^{2}.

For a prime
p
, the group
R_{N}^{2}
is
p
-divisible if and only if

p\mid N
. Since
p
-divisibility is preserved by group isomorphisms, we
must have
\mathcal{P}(N)=\mathcal{P}(M).

Thus
R_{N}=R_{M}.

Write this common group as
R
.

The isomorphism of unital Elliott invariants gives an order-unit preserving
group isomorphism
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus R\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus R

such that
\alpha_{0}(1,0)=(1,0)

and
\rho_{\theta^{\prime},M}\circ\alpha_{0}=\rho_{\theta,N}.

Since
\operatorname{Hom}(R,\mathbb{Z})=0,

every order-unit preserving automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus R
has the form
\alpha_{0}(a,q)=(a,uq)

for some unit
u\in R^{\times}
. The trace-compatibility condition gives
a+\theta^{\prime}uq=a+\theta q

for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q\in R
. Hence
\theta=\theta^{\prime}u,

or equivalently
\frac{\theta}{\theta^{\prime}}=u\in R_{N}^{\times}.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathcal{P}(N)=\mathcal{P}(M)

and
\theta=u\theta^{\prime}

for some
u\in R_{N}^{\times}
. Then
R_{N}=R_{M}=:R
.
Define
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus R\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus R
by
\alpha_{0}(a,q):=(a,uq).

This is a group isomorphism, preserves the order unit
(1,0)
, and satisfies

\rho_{\theta^{\prime},M}(\alpha_{0}(a,q))=\rho_{\theta^{\prime},M}(a,uq)=a+\theta^{\prime}uq=a+\theta q=\rho_{\theta,N}(a,q).

Therefore
\alpha_{0}
preserves the positive cones. On
K_{1}
, we may take
any group isomorphism
\alpha_{1}\colon R_{N}^{2}\to R_{M}^{2},

for example the identity after identifying
R_{N}=R_{M}
. Thus the unital
Elliott invariants of
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}
and
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},M}
are
isomorphic. By the classification theorem for the simple unital class considered
above,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,N}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},M}.

In particular, the algebra remembers the set of prime divisors of
N
, but
not the integer
N
itself. For example,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,2}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,4}
, whereas

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,2}\not\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta^{\prime},6}
for every irrational

\theta^{\prime}
, since
\mathcal{P}(2)\neq\mathcal{P}(6)
.

Example 3.6

(A pure-corner stable protorus and its classification)
.

Fix a nondegenerate skew-symmetric matrix
\Theta\in M_{d}(\mathbb{R})
. Put
m_{n}:=2^{n-1}
and
B_{n}^{\Theta}:=M_{m_{n}}(A_{\Theta})
,
for all
n\geq 1
. Define
\phi_{n}^{\Theta}\colon B_{n}^{\Theta}\longrightarrow B_{n+1}^{\Theta}=M_{2m_{n}}(A_{\Theta})

by the upper-left corner inclusion
\phi_{n}^{\Theta}(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Equivalently, if
p_{n}:=\begin{pmatrix}1_{B_{n}^{\Theta}}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in B_{n+1}^{\Theta},

then
\phi_{n}^{\Theta}
is the composite
B_{n}^{\Theta}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }p_{n}B_{n+1}^{\Theta}p_{n}\xrightarrow{\ \iota_{p_{n}}\ }B_{n+1}^{\Theta}.

Thus this is a pure-corner system. Define

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}:=\varinjlim(B_{n}^{\Theta},\phi_{n}^{\Theta}).

Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K}.

Indeed, identify
M_{m_{n}}(A_{\Theta})
with the corner
(1_{A_{\Theta}}\otimes e_{m_{n}})(A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K})(1_{A_{\Theta}}\otimes e_{m_{n}}),

where
e_{m_{n}}
is the rank-
m_{n}
projection onto the first
m_{n}
basis
vectors. Under this identification, the maps
\phi_{n}^{\Theta}
are precisely
the inclusions of these corners. Since
e_{m_{n}}\nearrow 1
strictly in
M(\mathcal{K})=B(\ell^{2})
, the union of these corners is dense in

A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K}
. Hence the inductive limit is
A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K}
.

Under the standard Morita identifications
K_{i}(B_{n}^{\Theta})\cong K_{i}(A_{\Theta}),

the maps
(\phi_{n}^{\Theta})_{*i}\colon K_{i}(B_{n}^{\Theta})\to K_{i}(B_{n+1}^{\Theta})

are the identity maps. Therefore

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})\cong K_{0}(A_{\Theta})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{d},

and
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})\cong K_{1}(A_{\Theta})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

The normalized trace on

B_{n}^{\Theta}=M_{m_{n}}(A_{\Theta})

is
\tau_{n}^{\Theta}=\frac{1}{m_{n}}\mathrm{Tr}_{m_{n}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta}=2^{-(n-1)}\mathrm{Tr}_{2^{n-1}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta},

where
\mathrm{Tr}_{m_{n}}
denotes the unnormalized matrix trace. For

a\in B_{n}^{\Theta}
, we have

\tau_{n+1}^{\Theta}(\phi_{n}^{\Theta}(a))=\frac{1}{2m_{n}}(\mathrm{Tr}_{2m_{n}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta})\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\,\tau_{n}^{\Theta}(a).

Thus
t_{n}=1/2
and
c_{n}=(t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1})^{-1}=2^{n-1}=m_{n}.

By Theorem
3.2
, the cone of densely defined lower
semicontinuous traces on
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}
is one-dimensional:
\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})=\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau^{\Theta},

where the distinguished generator
\tau^{\Theta}
is characterized by

\tau^{\Theta}\circ\iota_{n}=c_{n}\tau_{n}^{\Theta}=\mathrm{Tr}_{m_{n}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta}.

Under the identification
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K},

this trace is exactly
\tau_{\Theta}\otimes\mathrm{Tr},

where
\mathrm{Tr}
is the usual unbounded trace on
\mathcal{K}
.

Let
\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}}:=(\tau^{\Theta})_{*}\colon K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})\to\mathbb{R}.

Since, under the Morita identification
K_{0}(B_{n}^{\Theta})\cong K_{0}(A_{\Theta}),

one has
\rho_{n}^{\Theta}=\frac{1}{m_{n}}\rho_{\Theta},

the formula
\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}}\circ(\iota_{n})_{*}=c_{n}\rho_{n}^{\Theta}

gives
\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}}\circ(\iota_{n})_{*}=m_{n}\cdot\frac{1}{m_{n}}\rho_{\Theta}=\rho_{\Theta}.

Hence
\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}}(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}))=\rho_{\Theta}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta})).

The positive cone is transported by Morita equivalence. Therefore, under the
standard identification
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})\cong K_{0}(A_{\Theta}),

one has
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})^{+}=K_{0}(A_{\Theta})^{+}.

Equivalently
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})^{+}=\{x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}):\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}}(x)>0\}\cup\{0\}.

Since
c_{n}=2^{n-1}\to\infty,

Theorem
3.2
gives the full projection scale
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})^{+}.

Equivalently, every positive
K_{0}
-class is represented by a projection in
the stable algebra
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\Theta}\otimes\mathcal{K}.

The scale function is

\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}}(\lambda\tau^{\Theta})=\begin{cases}0,&\lambda=0,\\
+\infty,&\lambda>0.\end{cases}

Using the reduced stable form of the Elliott invariant, we obtain

\mathrm{Ell}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})=\left(K_{0}(A_{\Theta}),\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})=\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau^{\Theta},\rho_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{pro}},K_{1}(A_{\Theta})\right),

with full projection scale and infinite scale function on every nonzero trace.

We now classify these stable pure-corner protori by their Elliott invariants.
Let
\Psi\in M_{e}(\mathbb{R})
be another nondegenerate skew-symmetric matrix, and form
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}
in the same way. Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}

if and only if there exist graded group isomorphisms

\Gamma_{0}\colon K_{0}(A_{\Theta})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{0}(A_{\Psi}),\qquad\Gamma_{1}\colon K_{1}(A_{\Theta})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{1}(A_{\Psi}),

and a scalar
\lambda>0
such that
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\Gamma_{0}=\lambda\,\rho_{\Theta}.

Indeed, an isomorphism
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}

induces isomorphisms on
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
. Since the trace cones are both
one-dimensional rays, the induced affine homeomorphism of trace cones sends
the distinguished generator
\tau^{\Psi}
to
\lambda\tau^{\Theta}
for some

\lambda>0
. Compatibility of the Elliott invariant then gives
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\Gamma_{0}=\lambda\rho_{\Theta}.

Conversely, suppose such
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}
, and
\lambda
exist. Since
the positive cones are trace-determined, the relation
\rho_{\Psi}\circ\Gamma_{0}=\lambda\rho_{\Theta}

implies that
\Gamma_{0}
is an order isomorphism. The projection scales are
both full,

\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta})^{+},\qquad\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi})^{+},

and the scale functions are both infinite on nonzero traces. Therefore the data
\Gamma_{0},

\Gamma_{1},

\tau^{\Psi}\mapsto\lambda\tau^{\Theta}

give an isomorphism of the unified Elliott invariants. By the classification
theorem used in this section,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}.

In particular,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Theta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\Psi}
forces
\operatorname{rank}K_{0}(A_{\Theta})=\operatorname{rank}K_{0}(A_{\Psi}),

hence
2^{d-1}=2^{e-1},

so
d=e
. Thus the dimension of the original noncommutative torus is remembered
by the stable pure-corner protorus.

Notice that in dimension
2
, this criterion becomes the usual stable classification of
irrational rotation algebras. Namely, for irrational parameters

\theta,\eta
, one has
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{J(\theta)}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{J(\eta)}

if and only if there exist
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

and
\lambda>0
such that

(1,\eta)\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\lambda(1,\theta).

Equivalently,
\theta=\frac{b+d\eta}{a+c\eta}

with
a+c\eta>0

after possibly multiplying the matrix by
-1
.

Example 3.7

(A nonunital dimension-changing toric-corner model and its classification)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{j})_{j\geq 1}

be a sequence of irrational real numbers.
For
n\geq 1
, define
\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=J(\theta_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus J(\theta_{n})\in M_{2n}(\mathbb{R}),
where
J(\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\
-\alpha&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then each
\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is nondegenerate. Indeed, if
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
satisfies
\exp(2\pi i\langle x,\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}y\rangle)=1
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
,
then testing against the two standard basis vectors in each
J(\theta_{j})
-block
forces all coordinates of
x
to be zero, since each
\theta_{j}
is
irrational.

Put
B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=M_{2^{n-1}}(A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}),

and let
M_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2n+2}

be the inclusion of the first
2n
coordinates. Then

M_{n}^{t}\Theta_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}M_{n}=\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}.

Hence Proposition
2.4
gives a unital toric map
\varphi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\colon A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\longrightarrow A_{\Theta_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}

corresponding to
M_{n}
. Define
\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\colon B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\longrightarrow B_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

by

\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\bigl(\text{upper-left corner inclusion}\bigr)\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{M_{2^{n-1}}}\otimes\varphi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}).

Equivalently, after identifying
B_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=M_{2}\bigl(M_{2^{n-1}}(A_{\Theta_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\bigr),

one has

\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(a)=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathrm{Id}_{M_{2^{n-1}}}\otimes\varphi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})(a)&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Thus
\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a dimension-changing toric map followed
by a proper corner inclusion; it is a Case (3) map in the notation of
Proposition
3.3
. Define

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\varinjlim(B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}).

Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a simple nonunital protoral

C^{*}
-algebra. Simplicity follows from
Proposition
3.1
. To see that it is nonunital, let
e_{n}:=\iota_{n}(1_{B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})\in A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}.
The projections
e_{n}
form an increasing approximate unit. The trace computed
below satisfies
\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(e_{n})=2^{n-1}
, so the projections

e_{n}
are not eventually constant. If
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
were
unital, then
e_{n}\to 1
in norm; since the
e_{n}
are projections, this
would force
e_{n}=1
for all large
n
, a contradiction. Hence

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is nonunital.

We compute its
K
-theory. Under the standard Morita identifications
K_{i}(B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong K_{i}(A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}),
the upper-left corner inclusion contributes the identity on
K
-theory, while
the toric part contributes
\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})
on

K_{0}
and

\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})
on
K_{1}

by Proposition
3.3
. Therefore

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong\varinjlim\bigl(\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{2n},\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(M_{n})\bigr)\quad\text{and }\quad K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong\varinjlim\bigl(\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{Z}^{2n},\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(M_{n})\bigr).

Since
\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2n},M_{n})=\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}:=\bigoplus_{j\geq 1}\mathbb{Z}e_{j},

and exterior powers commute with filtered colimits, we obtain
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)})
and
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}).

Next consider traces. The normalized trace on
B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=M_{2^{n-1}}(A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}})

is
\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=2^{-(n-1)}\mathrm{Tr}_{2^{n-1}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}},

where
\mathrm{Tr}_{2^{n-1}}
denotes the unnormalized matrix trace. Since

\varphi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is unital and trace-preserving, and since the
upper-left corner has normalized trace
1/2
inside

B_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we have
\tau_{n+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\circ\phi_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}.

Thus
t_{n}=1/2
and
c_{n}=(t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1})^{-1}=2^{n-1}.

By Theorem
3.2
,
\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}},

where the distinguished generator
\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is characterized
by
\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\circ\iota_{n}=c_{n}\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{2^{n-1}}\otimes\tau_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}.

In particular,
\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(e_{n})=c_{n}=2^{n-1}.

Since
c_{n}\to\infty
, Theorem
3.2
gives the full
projection scale
\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}))=K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{+}.

The scale function is

\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(\lambda\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=\begin{cases}0,&\lambda=0,\\
+\infty,&\lambda>0.\end{cases}

We now compute the trace range. By Elliott’s trace formula for
noncommutative tori

[
13
, §1.3, Theorems 2.2 and 3.1]
,

\rho_{\Theta_{n}}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{n}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}))=\sum_{I\in\mathrm{Minor}(2n)}\operatorname{pf}((\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{I})\,\mathbb{Z}.

For the block-diagonal matrix
\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=J(\theta_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus J(\theta_{n}),
the only nonzero Pfaffian minors are the products

\prod_{j\in F}\theta_{j}
where
F\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
with the empty product interpreted as
1
.
Therefore

\rho_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}))=\left\langle\prod_{j\in F}\theta_{j}:F\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Passing from
A_{\Theta_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
to

B_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
divides the trace range by
2^{n-1}
, because

\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is the normalized matrix trace. But the
direct-limit trace satisfies
(\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{*}\circ(\iota_{n})_{*}=c_{n}(\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{*}=2^{n-1}(\tau_{n}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{*},

so the factor
2^{n-1}
exactly cancels the matrix normalization. Hence the
contribution from stage
n
is again

\left\langle\prod_{j\in F}\theta_{j}:F\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Taking the union over
n
, we get

(\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{*}(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}))=\left\langle\prod_{j\in F}\theta_{j}:F\subset\mathbb{N},\ |F|<\infty\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}},

with the empty product interpreted as
1
.

Equivalently, under the identification
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}),

the trace map
\rho_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}:=(\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})_{*}

is determined as follows. Write the underlying free abelian group as

\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}=\bigoplus_{j\geq 1}(\mathbb{Z}x_{j}\oplus\mathbb{Z}y_{j}),

where
x_{j},y_{j}
correspond to the
j
-th
J(\theta_{j})
-block. Then
\rho_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}(1)=1,

and, for
j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}
,
\rho_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\wedge y_{j_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge x_{j_{k}}\wedge y_{j_{k}}\bigr)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\theta_{j_{\ell}}.

The trace is zero on exterior basis monomials which are not, up to sign, wedges
of complete block pairs. Thus

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{+}=\{x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):\rho_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}(x)>0\}\cup\{0\}.

Finally, we record the Elliott-invariant classification of these examples. Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\eta_{j})_{j\geq 1}

be another sequence of irrational real numbers, and construct

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
in the same way. Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}

if and only if there exist graded group isomorphisms

\Gamma_{0}\colon\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}),

and
\Gamma_{1}\colon\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathbb{Z}^{(\infty)}),

together with a scalar
\lambda>0
, such that
\rho_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}\circ\Gamma_{0}=\lambda\,\rho_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{pro}}.

Indeed, an isomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras gives an isomorphism of the unified
Elliott invariants. Since both trace cones are one-dimensional, the induced
affine homeomorphism of trace cones sends

\tau^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
to

\lambda\tau^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

for some
\lambda>0
, and compatibility of the trace pairing gives the
displayed equation. Conversely, if such
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}
, and
\lambda

exist, then
\Gamma_{0}
is an order isomorphism because the positive cones are
trace-determined. The projection scales are both full and the scale functions
are both infinite on every nonzero trace. Hence these data give an isomorphism
of the unified Elliott invariants, and the classification theorem used in this
section gives
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}.

In particular, an isomorphism
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}

forces the trace ranges to agree up to positive scalar:

\left\langle\prod_{j\in F}\eta_{j}:F\subset\mathbb{N},\ |F|<\infty\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}=\lambda\left\langle\prod_{j\in F}\theta_{j}:F\subset\mathbb{N},\ |F|<\infty\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}

for some
\lambda>0
. Conversely, any block permutation of the sequence

(\theta_{j})
gives an isomorphic protorus, because the corresponding
permutation of the pairs
(x_{j},y_{j})
induces exterior-algebra isomorphisms
preserving the trace pairing.

Example 3.8

(An explicit same-dimensional noncorner family and its classification)
.

Fix an integer
N\geq 2
and an irrational number

\theta_{0}\in(0,1)
. Define recursively
\theta_{n+1}:=\frac{\theta_{n}}{N+\theta_{n}},
for

n\geq 0
, and put

\eta_{n}:=\frac{\theta_{n}}{N}.

Then
\eta_{n}=\frac{\theta_{n+1}}{1-\theta_{n+1}}.
Each
\theta_{n}
is irrational and lies in
(0,1)
.

Let
J(\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}0&\alpha\\
-\alpha&0\end{pmatrix}.

For each
n\geq 0
, consider the integer matrix
M_{N}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&N\end{pmatrix}.

Then
M_{N}^{t}J(\eta_{n})M_{N}=J(N\eta_{n})=J(\theta_{n}).

Hence Proposition
2.4
gives a unital toric map

\psi_{n}\colon A_{\theta_{n}}\longrightarrow A_{\eta_{n}}

determined on canonical generators by

\psi_{n}(U_{\theta_{n}})=U_{\eta_{n}},\qquad\psi_{n}(V_{\theta_{n}})=V_{\eta_{n}}^{\,N}.

Since
|\det(M_{N})|=N>1,

this toric step has proper range.

Next choose a projection
q_{n}\in A_{\theta_{n+1}}

with
\tau_{\theta_{n+1}}(q_{n})=1-\theta_{n+1}.

Equivalently, under the standard identification
K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n+1}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

\rho_{\theta_{n+1}}(a,b)=a+b\theta_{n+1},

we may take
[q_{n}]=(1,-1),

since
\rho_{\theta_{n+1}}(1,-1)=1-\theta_{n+1}>0.

By the standard Rieffel corner realization for irrational rotation algebras,
using
\eta_{n}=\frac{\theta_{n+1}}{1-\theta_{n+1}},

there is a unital isomorphism
\chi_{n}\colon A_{\eta_{n}}\xrightarrow{\cong}q_{n}A_{\theta_{n+1}}q_{n}.

We choose
\chi_{n}
so that, under the standard identifications

K_{0}(A_{\eta_{n}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n+1}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2},
given by
\rho_{\eta_{n}}(a,b)=a+b\eta_{n},
respectively

\rho_{\theta_{n+1}}(a,b)=a+b\theta_{n+1},

the induced map on
K_{0}
, after including the corner into

A_{\theta_{n+1}}
, is

(a,b)\longmapsto(a,-a+b).

We also choose
\chi_{n}
so that, under the standard Morita identification
K_{1}(q_{n}A_{\theta_{n+1}}q_{n})\cong K_{1}(A_{\theta_{n+1}}),

its induced map on
K_{1}
is the identity. This choice is compatible with the Elliott classification theorem for unital
simple AT algebras of real rank zero, since the displayed
K_{0}
-map and the
chosen
K_{1}
-map give an isomorphism of Elliott invariants of

A_{\eta_{n}}
and
q_{n}A_{\theta_{n+1}}q_{n}
.

Define
\phi_{n}:=\bigl(q_{n}A_{\theta_{n+1}}q_{n}\hookrightarrow A_{\theta_{n+1}}\bigr)\circ\chi_{n}\circ\psi_{n}\colon A_{\theta_{n}}\longrightarrow A_{\theta_{n+1}},

and set

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}:=\varinjlim(A_{\theta_{n}},\phi_{n}).

Then each
\phi_{n}
is a same-dimensional nonunital noncorner embedding of
Case (4) in Proposition
3.3
. It is
nonunital because
\phi_{n}(1_{A_{\theta_{n}}})=q_{n}\neq 1_{A_{\theta_{n+1}}},

and the range inside the corner is proper because the toric matrix has
|\det(M_{N})|=N>1.

We now compute the invariant. Identify
K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

by
\rho_{\theta_{n}}(a,b)=a+b\theta_{n}.

The toric step
\psi_{n}
induces
(\psi_{n})_{*0}(a,b)=(a,Nb),

because, on
K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n}})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{2},

the degree-two generator is multiplied by
\det(M_{N})=N.

The corner step contributes
(a,b)\longmapsto(a,-a+b)

on
K_{0}
. Indeed, the normalized trace on the corner is

x\longmapsto\frac{\rho_{\theta_{n+1}}(x)}{1-\theta_{n+1}},

and this agrees with
\rho_{\eta_{n}}
since

\rho_{\theta_{n+1}}(0,1)(1-\theta_{n+1})^{-1}={\theta_{n+1}}(1-\theta_{n+1})^{-1}=\eta_{n}.
Consequently
(\phi_{n})_{*0}(a,b)=(a,-a+Nb).

Set
s_{0}:=0
,
s_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}N^{-k},
and
D_{N}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&N\end{pmatrix}.

Thus

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})\cong\varinjlim\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\xrightarrow{\ D_{N}\ }\mathbb{Z}^{2}\xrightarrow{\ D_{N}\ }\mathbb{Z}^{2}\xrightarrow{\ D_{N}\ }\cdots\right).

For each
n\geq 0
, define

F_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N]

by
F_{n}(a,b):=\left(a,\frac{b}{N^{n}}+a\,s_{n}\right).

A direct computation gives
F_{n+1}\bigl(D_{N}(a,b)\bigr)=F_{n}(a,b).
Therefore the maps
F_{n}
induce a homomorphism
\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},D_{N})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N].

This homomorphism is an isomorphism. Indeed, each
F_{n}
is injective, and
every element
(m,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N]
has the form
F_{n}(a,b)
for all
sufficiently large
n
, by taking
a=m
and
b=N^{n}(q-ms_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}.
Hence
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N].

For
K_{1}
, the toric step contributes
(a,b)\longmapsto(a,Nb),

and our choice of
\chi_{n}
makes the corner-identification step the identity
on
K_{1}
, after Morita identification. Hence

K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})\cong\varinjlim\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&N\end{pmatrix}\right)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N].

We index this system from
n=0
. Let
e_{n}\in A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}

denote the image of
1_{A_{\theta_{n}}}
. The trace-scaling constant is
t_{n}=\tau_{\theta_{n+1}}(\phi_{n}(1))=\tau_{\theta_{n+1}}(q_{n})=1-\theta_{n+1}.

Normalize the trace ray by setting
c_{0}:=1
,
c_{n+1}:=\frac{c_{n}}{t_{n}}.

Using
\theta_{n+1}=\frac{\theta_{n}}{N+\theta_{n}},

one checks inductively that
c_{n}=1+\theta_{0}s_{n}
and
c_{n}\theta_{n}=\frac{\theta_{0}}{N^{n}}.
For
(a,b)\in K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the direct-limit trace is therefore

c_{n}\rho_{\theta_{n}}(a,b)=c_{n}(a+b\theta_{n})=a+\theta_{0}\left(\frac{b}{N^{n}}+a\,s_{n}\right).

Thus, under the identification

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N],

the trace is
\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}(m,q)=m+\theta_{0}q.

Since each
c_{n}
is positive, one has

(a,b)\in K_{0}(A_{\theta_{n}})^{+}\,\Leftrightarrow\,\rho_{\theta_{n}}(a,b)>0\,\Leftrightarrow\,\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}(F_{n}(a,b))>0.

It follows that

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})^{+}=\{(m,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[1/N]:m+\theta_{0}q>0\}\cup\{0\}.

The trace cone is one-dimensional:
\widetilde{T}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})=\mathbb{R}_{+}\tau_{N,\theta_{0}},

where
\tau_{N,\theta_{0}}
is normalized by
\tau_{N,\theta_{0}}(e_{0})=1.

The class of the stage-
n
unit is
[e_{n}]=F_{n}(1,0)=\left(1,\sum_{k=1}^{n}N^{-k}\right).

By Theorem
3.2
, the projection scale is

\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}))=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}[0,[e_{n}]].

Moreover,
\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}([e_{n}])=1+\theta_{0}\sum_{k=1}^{n}N^{-k}\nearrow 1+\frac{\theta_{0}}{N-1}.

Since the order on
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})
is determined by

\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}
, we obtain

\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}))=\left\{x\in K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})^{+}:\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}(x)<1+\frac{\theta_{0}}{N-1}\right\}.

Equivalently, the scale function is
\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}}(\lambda\tau_{N,\theta_{0}})=\lambda\left(1+\frac{\theta_{0}}{N-1}\right),
for
\lambda\in[0,\infty).

In particular,
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}
is nonunital: the projections

(e_{n})
form an increasing approximate unit, but their
K_{0}
-classes
[e_{n}]=\left(1,\sum_{k=1}^{n}N^{-k}\right)

are not eventually constant.

We now record the classification of these examples by the Elliott invariant.
Let
M\geq 2
and let
\theta_{0}^{\prime}\in(0,1)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, and construct

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}
in the same way. Put
R_{N}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/N],

R_{M}:=\mathbb{Z}[1/M],
and
L_{N,\theta_{0}}:=1+\frac{\theta_{0}}{N-1},

L_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}:=1+\frac{\theta_{0}^{\prime}}{M-1}.

Also let
\mathcal{P}(N):=\{p\text{ prime}:p\mid N\}.

Then

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}

if and only if the following conditions hold:

\mathcal{P}(N)=\mathcal{P}(M),

and, writing
R:=R_{N}=R_{M},

there exist
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
,
v\in R
,
u\in R^{\times}
, and

\lambda>0
,
such that

\lambda=\varepsilon+\theta_{0}^{\prime}v,

\theta_{0}^{\prime}u=\lambda\theta_{0}
, and
L_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}=\lambda L_{N,\theta_{0}}.

Indeed, suppose first that
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}.

Then
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}})\cong K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}),

so
\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{N}\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{M}.

For a prime
p
, the subgroup
\bigcap_{k\geq 1}p^{k}(\mathbb{Z}\oplus R_{N})

is equal to
0\oplus R_{N}
if
p\mid N
, and is zero if
p\nmid N
.
This is an isomorphism invariant of the abelian group. Hence
\mathcal{P}(N)=\mathcal{P}(M),

and therefore
R_{N}=R_{M}=:R.

The induced isomorphism on
K_{0}
is a group automorphism
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus R\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus R.
Since
\operatorname{Hom}(R,\mathbb{Z})=0,

every group automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus R
has the form
\alpha_{0}(a,q)=(\varepsilon a,va+uq),

where
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\},

v\in R
, and
u\in R^{\times}
.

The trace cones are one-dimensional. Hence the affine homeomorphism of trace
cones sends
\tau_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}
to
\lambda\tau_{N,\theta_{0}}

for some
\lambda>0
. Compatibility of the trace pairing gives
\rho_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{0}(a,q))=\lambda\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}(a,q)

for every
(a,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus R
. In other words,
\varepsilon a+\theta_{0}^{\prime}(va+uq)=\lambda(a+\theta_{0}q).

Comparing coefficients gives

\lambda=\varepsilon+\theta_{0}^{\prime}v
and
\theta_{0}^{\prime}u=\lambda\theta_{0}.

Compatibility of the scale functions gives
L_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}=\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}}(\tau_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}})=\Sigma_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}}(\lambda\tau_{N,\theta_{0}})=\lambda L_{N,\theta_{0}}.

Conversely, suppose that the displayed conditions hold. Define
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus R\to\mathbb{Z}\oplus R
by

\alpha_{0}(a,q):=(\varepsilon a,va+uq).

Then
\alpha_{0}
is a group isomorphism and

\rho_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{0}(a,q))=\lambda\rho_{N,\theta_{0}}(a,q).

Hence
\alpha_{0}
is an order isomorphism. The equation

L_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}=\lambda L_{N,\theta_{0}}

says exactly that the scale functions are preserved by the trace-cone map
\tau_{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}\longmapsto\lambda\tau_{N,\theta_{0}}.

Equivalently, the projection scales are also preserved:
\alpha_{0}\bigl(\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}))\bigr)=\Sigma(K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}})).

On
K_{1}
, choose any group isomorphism
\alpha_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}\oplus R\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\oplus R.

Thus the unified Elliott invariants of

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}
and
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. By the
classification theorem used in this section,

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{N,\theta_{0}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{M,\theta_{0}^{\prime}}.

Example 3.9

(A unital non-toric
K_{1}
-engine and its classification)
.

Fix an irrational number
\theta\in(0,1)
, and let
\mathbf{P}=(P_{n})_{n\geq 1},

P_{n}\in M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
be any sequence of integer matrices. For every
n\geq 1
, put

B_{n}:=A_{\theta}
. We use the standard identifications
K_{0}(A_{\theta})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
\rho_{\theta}(a,b)=a+b\theta
,

[1_{A_{\theta}}]=(1,0)
,
and
K_{1}(A_{\theta})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Define graded group homomorphisms
\kappa_{0,n}:=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\colon K_{0}(A_{\theta})\to K_{0}(A_{\theta})
and
\kappa_{1,n}:=P_{n}\colon K_{1}(A_{\theta})\to K_{1}(A_{\theta}).
Since
\kappa_{0,n}([1])=[1]
and
\rho_{\theta}\circ\kappa_{0,n}=\rho_{\theta}
, Theorem
2.2
gives a unital monomorphism
\phi_{n}\colon A_{\theta}\to A_{\theta}

such that
(\phi_{n})_{*0}=\kappa_{0,n}
and

(\phi_{n})_{*1}=P_{n}.
Define

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}:=\varinjlim(A_{\theta},\phi_{n}).

Then
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}
is a simple unital noncommutative
protorus.

The invariant is immediate from Theorem
3.2
. Since the
connecting maps are unital, the limit is unital and has a unique tracial state

\tau_{\theta,\mathbf{P}}
. On
K_{0}
, all connecting maps are the identity,
so
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
with order unit
[1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}}]=(1,0)
,
positive cone
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})^{+}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a+b\theta>0\}\cup\{0\}
,
and trace pairing
\rho_{\theta,\mathbf{P}}(a,b):=(\tau_{\theta,\mathbf{P}})_{*}(a,b)=a+b\theta.

On
K_{1}
,

K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})\cong\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},P_{n}).

For example, if
P_{n}=0
for every
n
, then
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})=0.

If, for a fixed integer
N\geq 2
,
P_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}N&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

for every
n
, then
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})\cong\mathbb{Z}[1/N].

We now record the classification of this family by the Elliott invariant. Let

\eta\in(0,1)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathbf{Q}=(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
Q_{n}\in M_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
and construct
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\eta,\mathbf{Q}}
in the same way. Put
G_{\mathbf{P}}:=\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},P_{n})
and
G_{\mathbf{Q}}:=\varinjlim(\mathbb{Z}^{2},Q_{n}).

Then

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\eta,\mathbf{Q}}

as unital
C^{*}
-algebras if and only if

G_{\mathbf{P}}\cong G_{\mathbf{Q}}

as abelian groups and there exist
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}

such that
\theta=r+\varepsilon\eta.

Since
\theta,\eta\in(0,1)
, this last condition is equivalently
\theta=\eta
or
\theta=1-\eta
.

Indeed, an isomorphism
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\eta,\mathbf{Q}}

induces an isomorphism
G_{\mathbf{P}}\cong G_{\mathbf{Q}}

on
K_{1}
. On
K_{0}
, it induces an order-unit preserving group automorphism
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}

satisfying
\rho_{\eta,\mathbf{Q}}\circ\alpha_{0}=\rho_{\theta,\mathbf{P}}.

Every automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
preserving
(1,0)
has the form
\alpha_{0}(a,b)=(a+rb,\varepsilon b),
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}.

Trace compatibility gives
a+rb+\varepsilon\eta b=a+\theta b

for all
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, hence
\theta=r+\varepsilon\eta.

Conversely, if
G_{\mathbf{P}}\cong G_{\mathbf{Q}}
and

\theta=r+\varepsilon\eta
, then
\alpha_{0}(a,b):=(a+rb,\varepsilon b)

is an order-unit preserving
K_{0}
-isomorphism compatible with traces, and any
chosen isomorphism
G_{\mathbf{P}}\to G_{\mathbf{Q}}

gives the
K_{1}
-part. Thus the unital Elliott invariants are isomorphic, and
the classification theorem gives
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\eta,\mathbf{Q}}.

These systems are generally not produced by the four explicit toric-corner
classes. For instance, if
P_{n}=0
for every
n
, then each connecting map
has zero
K_{1}
-map. This cannot occur for the toric-corner maps of
Cases (1)–(4) in the simple full-rank setting: after the
Morita-identification part, those maps induce exterior-algebra maps coming from
full-rank integer matrices, and hence are injective after tensoring with

\mathbb{Q}
on
K_{1}
. Consequently, a direct limit built from such maps has
nonzero rational
K_{1}
-rank, whereas the present choice
P_{n}=0
gives
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\theta,\mathbf{P}})=0.

Example 3.10

(A four-dimensional infinitesimal-killing protorus and its classification)
.

Choose irrational numbers
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)

such that
1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha\beta

are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
, and set
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}:=J(\alpha)\oplus J(\beta)\in M_{4}(\mathbb{R})
, where

J(t):=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\
-t&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then
A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}
is simple. Indeed, the irrationality of
\alpha
and
\beta
implies that the bicharacter associated to

\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}
is nondegenerate.

Under the exterior-algebra identification
K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})\cong\Lambda^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{Z}^{4},

write the standard even basis as

1,\quad e_{12},e_{13},e_{14},e_{23},e_{24},e_{34},\quad e_{1234}.

For the block-diagonal matrix
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}=J(\alpha)\oplus J(\beta),

Elliott’s trace formula gives
\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(1)=1,

\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{12})=\alpha,

\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{34})=\beta,

\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{1234})=\alpha\beta,
and
\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{13})=\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{14})=\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{23})=\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}(e_{24})=0.

Let
H_{\alpha,\beta}:=\langle 1,e_{12},e_{34},e_{1234}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{4}

and
L_{\alpha,\beta}:=\langle e_{13},e_{14},e_{23},e_{24}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{4}.

Then
K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})=H_{\alpha,\beta}\oplus L_{\alpha,\beta}

and

L_{\alpha,\beta}\subseteq\ker\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}.

Since
1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha\beta
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
, the
trace is injective on
H_{\alpha,\beta}
, and
L_{\alpha,\beta}
is exactly
the trace-zero summand in this decomposition.

Define
\kappa_{0}\colon K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})\longrightarrow K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})

by
\kappa_{0}|_{H_{\alpha,\beta}}=\mathrm{Id}_{H_{\alpha,\beta}}
and

\kappa_{0}|_{L_{\alpha,\beta}}=0.

Define also
\kappa_{1}:=0\colon K_{1}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})\longrightarrow K_{1}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}).

Then
\kappa_{0}([1])=[1]
and
\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}\circ\kappa_{0}=\rho_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}.

Moreover, because the positive cone is determined by the unique trace,

\kappa_{0}
sends every nonzero positive
K_{0}
-class to a nonzero positive

K_{0}
-class. Hence Theorem
2.2
gives a unital monomorphism
\phi_{\alpha,\beta}\colon A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}\longrightarrow A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}

such that
(\phi_{\alpha,\beta})_{*0}=\kappa_{0}
and

(\phi_{\alpha,\beta})_{*1}=0.
Define

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta}:=\varinjlim\bigl(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}},\phi_{\alpha,\beta}\bigr).

This is a simple unital protoral
C^{*}
-algebra. Since
\kappa_{0}
is an
idempotent projection with image
H_{\alpha,\beta}
, we have

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta})\cong\varinjlim(K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}}),\kappa_{0})\cong H_{\alpha,\beta}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{4}.

Using the ordered basis
1,\ e_{12},\ e_{34},\ e_{1234}

of
H_{\alpha,\beta}
, the trace pairing is
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathrm{pro}}(a,b,c,d)=a+b\alpha+c\beta+d\alpha\beta.

Thus

K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta})^{+}=\{(a,b,c,d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}:a+b\alpha+c\beta+d\alpha\beta>0\}\cup\{0\},

and the order unit is

[1_{A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta}}]=(1,0,0,0).

Since the connecting map on
K_{1}
is zero,
K_{1}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta})=0
. The system is unital, so
T(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta})=\{\tau_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathrm{pro}}\},

where
\tau_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathrm{pro}}
is the unique tracial state.

We now classify these examples by their Elliott invariants. Let
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in(0,1)

be irrational numbers such that
1,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}

are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Z}
, and construct

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
in the same way. Then

A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}

if and only if
\langle 1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha\beta\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}=\langle 1,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}

as subgroups of
\mathbb{R}
.

Indeed, the trace map identifies
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta})

order-isomorphically with the ordered subgroup
G_{\alpha,\beta}:=\langle 1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha\beta\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}

with order inherited from
\mathbb{R}
and distinguished order unit
1
. Similarly,
K_{0}(A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})

is identified with
G_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}:=\langle 1,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Since both algebras have
K_{1}=0
and unique tracial state, their unital
Elliott invariants are isomorphic exactly when

G_{\alpha,\beta}=G_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}

as ordered subgroups of
\mathbb{R}
carrying the same distinguished unit
1
.
By the classification theorem, this is equivalent to
A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha,\beta}\cong A_{\mathrm{pro}}^{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}.

Equivalently, the same condition can be written in matrix form: there exists
G\in\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})

with
G(1,0,0,0)^{t}=(1,0,0,0)^{t}

such that
(1,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime})\,G=(1,\alpha,\beta,\alpha\beta).

Finally, the connecting map
\phi_{\alpha,\beta}
is not of any of
Cases (1)–(4). A toric-corner map has, up to the

K
-theory isomorphism coming from the Morita/corner part, a
K
-theory
map of the form
\Lambda^{*}(M)
for a full-column-rank integer matrix
M
.
After tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
, such exterior maps are injective. The map

\kappa_{0}
above kills the rank-four subgroup

L_{\alpha,\beta}\subset K_{0}(A_{\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}})
, and

\kappa_{1}=0
. Hence this connecting map cannot arise from any of the
exterior-algebra maps appearing in Cases (1), (3), or
(4), nor from a pure corner isomorphism in Case (2).

4.
Spectral triples on noncommutative protori

In this last section, we construct spectral triples on the protoral

C^{*}
-algebras of Section
3
by means of the
inductive-limit construction of spectral triples from
[
21
]
. In the unital toric
case, namely Case (1) of Section
2.2
, the connecting maps act
directly on Fourier labels, so one can use the inductive realization of
[
21
]
for compatible families of Dirac operators. In the
nonunital cases, namely Cases (2), (3), and (4), the
connecting maps factor through full corners and are no longer unital. In that
regime we adapt the same Hilbert-space/operator construction using
trace-rescaled GNS maps, but the resulting objects are naturally locally
compact spectral triples rather than unital spectral triples.

4.1.
The unital case: Fourier-compatible spectral triples

We briefly recall the part of
[
21
]
that we use. A
spectral triple
(A,H,D)
on a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
A
consists of a
faithful unital representation
\pi\colon A\to\mathcal{B}(H)
and a
selfadjoint operator
D
such that
D
has compact resolvent and the

*
-algebra

A^{\infty}(D):=\{a\in A:[D,\pi(a)]\text{ is densely defined and extends to a bounded operator}\}

is dense in
A
.

An isometric morphism between two spectral triples
(A_{1},H_{1},D_{1})
and
(A_{2},H_{2},D_{2})
, in the sense of

[
21
, Definition 2.1]
, is a pair
(\phi,I)
, where

\phi\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
is an injective unital
*
-homomorphism and

I\colon H_{1}\to H_{2}
is an isometry, such that
\phi(A_{1}^{\infty}(D_{1}))\subseteq A_{2}^{\infty}(D_{2}),

I\pi_{1}(a)=\pi_{2}(\phi(a))I,

I(\operatorname{Dom}(D_{1}))\subseteq\operatorname{Dom}(D_{2})
, and
ID_{1}=D_{2}I
on
\operatorname{Dom}(D_{1})
. Given an inductive
system of spectral triples
\bigl\{(A_{j},H_{j},D_{j}),(\phi_{j,k},I_{j,k})\bigr\},

its inductive realization is the triple

(A:=\varinjlim A_{j},H:=\varinjlim H_{j},D:=\varinjlim D_{j}),

obtained by taking the Hilbert-space inductive limit and then closing the densely
defined operator
D(I_{j}\xi_{j}):=I_{j}D_{j}\xi_{j}

on the core
\bigcup_{j}I_{j}(\operatorname{Dom}(D_{j}))
; see

[
21
, Definition 2.3]
. Theorem 3.1 of
[
21
]
gives equivalent
criteria for the compactness of the resolvent of the limit operator, while
Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.2.1 show that the density of
A^{\infty}(D)
in
A
follows from uniform boundedness of the stagewise commutators
\bigl\{[D_{k},\pi_{k}(\phi_{j,k}(a))]\bigr\}_{k\geq j}

for
a\in A_{j}^{\infty}
.

Let now
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
be skew-symmetric. Write

A_{\Theta}^{\infty}=\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}a_{x}U^{x}:(a_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\text{ is Schwartz on }\mathbb{Z}^{m}\right\}

for the smooth subalgebra for the canonical
\mathbb{T}^{m}
-action, and write
A_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{alg}}:=\operatorname{span}\{U^{x}:x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\}

for the algebraic Fourier subalgebra. Under the twisted-group picture,

A_{\Theta}\cong C^{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{m},\sigma_{\Theta})
. Since
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is amenable, the
left regular representation is faithful. The GNS representation associated to
the canonical trace
\tau_{\Theta}
identifies
L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})\cong\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{m}),

and we denote by
\delta_{x}
the basis vector corresponding to the Fourier
monomial
U^{x}
.

For
1\leq j\leq m
, let
\delta_{j}^{\Theta}
be the canonical smooth

*
-derivation
\delta_{j}^{\Theta}(U^{x})=2\pi i\,x_{j}U^{x},
for

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}.

As an unbounded operator on
L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})
, the closure of

\delta_{j}^{\Theta}
is skew-adjoint. We put
P_{j}^{\Theta}:=-i\delta_{j}^{\Theta},

so that
P_{j}^{\Theta}(U^{x})=2\pi x_{j}U^{x}.

Fix a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
S
.
Let
F\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}(S)_{\mathrm{sa}}

be a selfadjoint matrix-valued function, and define Fourier-multiplier operator

D_{F}(\delta_{x}\otimes\xi)=\delta_{x}\otimes F(x)\xi,\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m},\quad\xi\in S,

on
H_{\Theta}:=L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})\otimes S
.

The following proposition is a twisted

\mathbb{Z}^{m}
-version of the standard construction of spectral triples from
proper translation-bounded length functions on discrete groups.

Proposition 4.1

(Fourier-multiplier triples on a single torus)
.

Assume that
F
satisfies the following two conditions:

(a)

[finite spectral multiplicity in bounded intervals]:
for every

R>0
, the vector space

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
\operatorname{Spec}(F(x))\cap[-R,R]\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}S

is finite-dimensional;

(b)

[bounded translation increments]:
for every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\|F(x+a)-F(x)\|<\infty.

Then
(A_{\Theta},H_{\Theta},D_{F})

is a spectral triple.

Proof.

Via the Fourier basis, we identify
H_{\Theta}=L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})\otimes S\cong\bigoplus_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}(\delta_{x}\otimes S).

With respect to this orthogonal decomposition, the operator
D_{F}
is the
diagonal direct sum

D_{F}=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}F(x),

with domain
\mathrm{dom}(D_{F})=\left\{(\xi_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\in\bigoplus_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}S:\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\|F(x)\xi_{x}\|^{2}<\infty\right\}.

Since each
F(x)
is selfadjoint on the finite-dimensional space
S
, the
operator
D_{F}
is selfadjoint. Indeed, for
\eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\in H_{\Theta}
, define
\xi_{x}:=(F(x)+i)^{-1}\eta_{x}.

Because
F(x)
is selfadjoint, one has

\|(F(x)+i)^{-1}\|\leq 1
and

\|F(x)(F(x)+i)^{-1}\|\leq 1.
Hence
\sum_{x}\|\xi_{x}\|^{2}\leq\sum_{x}\|\eta_{x}\|^{2}
and
\sum_{x}\|F(x)\xi_{x}\|^{2}\leq\sum_{x}\|\eta_{x}\|^{2}.

Thus
\xi\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{F})
, and
(D_{F}+i)\xi=\eta.

So
D_{F}+i
is surjective; similarly
D_{F}-i
is surjective. Therefore

D_{F}
is selfadjoint.

We now prove compact resolvent. For
R>0
, let

X_{R}:=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\operatorname{Spec}(F(x))\cap[-R,R]\neq\varnothing\}.

Because
S
is finite-dimensional, condition (a) is equivalent to the
finiteness of
X_{R}
. Let
E_{R}:=1_{[-R,R]}(D_{F})

be the spectral projection of
D_{F}
for the interval
[-R,R]
. Since
D_{F}

is diagonal, one has
E_{R}=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}1_{[-R,R]}(F(x)),

where
1_{[-R,R]}(F(x))
is the spectral projection of the finite-dimensional
selfadjoint operator
F(x)
. If
x\notin X_{R}
, then
1_{[-R,R]}(F(x))=0.

Hence

E_{R}=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{R}}1_{[-R,R]}(F(x)).

Since
X_{R}
is finite and each summand acts on the finite-dimensional space

S
, the projection
E_{R}
has finite rank. This implies compact resolvent. Indeed,

(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}=(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}E_{R}+(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}(1-E_{R}).

The first term is finite-rank because
E_{R}
is finite-rank. On the orthogonal
complement of
E_{R}
, the spectrum of
|D_{F}|
is contained in
(R,\infty)
,
so

\|(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}(1-E_{R})\|\leq(1+R^{2})^{-1/2}.

As
R\to\infty
, this tends to
0
. Therefore
(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}
is the
norm limit of finite-rank operators, hence is compact.

Conversely, if
(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}
is compact, then for every
R>0
,

E_{R}=1_{[(1+R^{2})^{-1/2},\,1]}\bigl((1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}\bigr).

Since
(1+D_{F}^{2})^{-1/2}
is a compact selfadjoint operator, its spectral
projection for any interval of the form
[\varepsilon,1]
with
\varepsilon>0

has finite rank. Hence
E_{R}
is finite-rank. Thus the two formulations are
equivalent.

It remains to verify bounded commutators. Let
\pi_{\Theta}
be the left regular
representation of
A_{\Theta}
on
H_{\Theta}
. For
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, left
multiplication by
U^{a}
is a twisted shift:

\pi_{\Theta}(U^{a})(\delta_{x}\otimes\xi)=\sigma_{\Theta}(a,x)\,\delta_{a+x}\otimes\xi,

where

\sigma_{\Theta}(a,x)\in\mathbb{T}
. Hence
[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(U^{a})](\delta_{x}\otimes\xi)=\sigma_{\Theta}(a,x)\,\delta_{a+x}\otimes\bigl(F(a+x)-F(x)\bigr)\xi.

Therefore

\|[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(U^{a})](\delta_{x}\otimes\xi)\|\leq\|F(a+x)-F(x)\|\,\|\xi\|.

By condition (b),
M_{a}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\|F(x+a)-F(x)\|<\infty,

so
\|[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(U^{a})]\|\leq M_{a}.

Thus
[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(U^{a})]
extends boundedly for every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. By
linearity, the same is true for every
b\in A_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{alg}}=\operatorname{span}\{U^{a}:a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\}.

Hence

A_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{alg}}\subseteq A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F}).

Since
A_{\Theta}^{\mathrm{alg}}
is dense in
A_{\Theta}
, the algebra

A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F})
is dense in
A_{\Theta}
. Therefore
(A_{\Theta},H_{\Theta},D_{F})

is a spectral triple.
∎

Observation 4.2

(Standard families in the unital regime)
.

We highlight the scope of Proposition
4.1
through a collection of classical examples and related constructions. Items (1) and (2) below give the fundamental examples, while Items (3) and (4) present further constructions derived from spectral triples arising from Proposition
4.1
. In general, these latter two constructions are no longer diagonal Fourier multipliers, so conditions (a) and (b) cease to be the relevant assumptions. We describe the precise mechanism involved in each case.

(1) [
Flat triples.
] Take
S=S_{m}
to be a complex Clifford module with selfadjoint Clifford
generators
\gamma_{1}^{(m)},\ldots,\gamma_{m}^{(m)}
, and define
F_{L}(x):=2\pi\,\gamma^{(m)}(Lx),
for some
L\in\mathrm{GL}(m,\mathbb{R})
,
where
\gamma^{(m)}(v):=\sum_{a=1}^{m}v_{a}\gamma_{a}^{(m)}.

Then

D_{\Theta,L}=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}L_{aj}P_{j}^{\Theta}\right)\otimes\gamma_{a}^{(m)}

is the standard flat translation-invariant Dirac operator associated to the cotangent norm

v\mapsto\|Lv\|
.

To check condition (a) of Proposition
4.1
, note that Clifford multiplication satisfies

\gamma^{(m)}(v)^{2}=\|v\|^{2}\,1_{S},

hence
\operatorname{Spec}(F_{L}(x))\subseteq\{\pm 2\pi\|Lx\|\}.

Therefore
\operatorname{Spec}(F_{L}(x))\cap[-R,R]\neq\varnothing
if and only if

\|Lx\|\leq\frac{R}{2\pi}.

Since
L
is invertible, there exists
c_{L}>0
such that
\|Lx\|\geq c_{L}\|x\|
for all

x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,
so
\|Lx\|\leq\frac{R}{2\pi}
implies
\|x\|\leq\frac{R}{2\pi c_{L}}.

Hence only finitely many
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
contribute, and (a) holds.

To check condition (b), observe that
F_{L}(x+a)-F_{L}(x)=2\pi\,\gamma^{(m)}(La),

which is independent of
x
. Thus

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\|F_{L}(x+a)-F_{L}(x)\|=2\pi\,\|\gamma^{(m)}(La)\|=2\pi\,\|La\|<\infty.

So (b) also holds.

These are the usual flat triples on smooth noncommutative tori; see

[
7
, Section 2]
and
[
8
, Section 3]
.

(2) [
Length-type triples.
]
Take
S=\mathbb{C}
and
F(x)=\ell(x)
, where
\ell\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to[0,\infty)

is a proper length function with bounded translation increments. Then

D_{\ell}\delta_{x}=\ell(x)\delta_{x}.

This is the twisted-group-algebra spectral triple associated to the
presentation
A_{\Theta}\cong C^{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{m},\sigma_{\Theta}).

Condition (a) is exactly properness of
\ell
: since
S=\mathbb{C}
,

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
\operatorname{Spec}(F(x))\cap[-R,R]\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mathbb{C}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
\ell(x)\leq R\end{subarray}}\mathbb{C},

which is finite-dimensional if and only if the set
\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\ell(x)\leq R\}

is finite.

Condition (b) is the bounded translation increment hypothesis:

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}|\ell(x+a)-\ell(x)|<\infty
for all

a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.
In particular, if
\ell
is a genuine group length in the usual sense, then

|\ell(x+a)-\ell(x)|\leq\ell(a),

so (b) is automatic.

For length-function Dirac operators on group
C^{*}
-algebras and their
twisted analogues, including noncommutative tori, see

[
39
, Introduction and Section 1]
; see also

[
1
]
for matrix-valued length-function triples.

(3) [
Bounded perturbations and inner fluctuations.
]
Let
(A_{\Theta},H_{\Theta},D_{F})
be a spectral triple obtained from
Proposition
4.1
, and let
B=B^{*}\in\mathcal{B}(H_{\Theta})

be bounded. Then
D_{F}+B
, with domain
\mathrm{dom}(D_{F})
, is again selfadjoint by
the Kato–Rellich theorem. Since
B
is bounded,
D_{F}+B
has compact
resolvent whenever
D_{F}
does. Moreover, for every
a\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F}),

one has
[D_{F}+B,\pi_{\Theta}(a)]=[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(a)]+[B,\pi_{\Theta}(a)],

and the second term is bounded because
B
and
\pi_{\Theta}(a)
are bounded.
Hence
A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F}+B)=A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F}).

In particular, one may consider inner fluctuations. The Connes one-forms
associated to
(A_{\Theta},H_{\Theta},D_{F})
are

\Omega^{1}_{D_{F}}(A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F})):=\left\{\sum_{j}\pi_{\Theta}(a_{j})[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(b_{j})]:a_{j},b_{j}\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F})\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(H_{\Theta}).

If
A=A^{*}\in\Omega^{1}_{D_{F}}(A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F}))
, then the fluctuated Dirac
operator

D_{F}^{A}:=D_{F}+A

is again a spectral triple on
A_{\Theta}
. This is the standard mechanism of
inner fluctuations in noncommutative geometry; see

[
11
, Chapter VI]
.

If one wants to remain literally inside the class of Fourier multipliers from
Proposition
4.1
, one may restrict to
diagonal bounded perturbations: let
G\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\operatorname{End}(S)_{\mathrm{sa}}

be bounded,
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\|G(x)\|<\infty,

and suppose that
G
has bounded translation increments. Then

(F+G)(x):=F(x)+G(x)

again satisfies (a) and (b). Indeed, boundedness of
G

implies that for every
R>0
,
\operatorname{Spec}(F(x)+G(x))\cap[-R,R]\neq\varnothing

can occur only when

\operatorname{Spec}(F(x))\cap[-(R+\|G\|_{\infty}),\,R+\|G\|_{\infty}]\neq\varnothing,

so (a) follows from the corresponding property for
F
. Condition
(b) follows from

\|(F+G)(x+a)-(F+G)(x)\|\leq\|F(x+a)-F(x)\|+\|G(x+a)-G(x)\|.

(4) [
Conformal or curved triples.
]
Conformal or curved Dirac operators are generally not direct Fourier
multipliers, so conditions (a) and (b) of
Proposition
4.1
do not literally apply
to them. Rather, they should be viewed as deformations of spectral triples
already constructed by Fourier-multiplier or flat methods.

A standard conformal deformation starts with a positive invertible element
k=e^{h/2}\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}

and a flat or Fourier-multiplier spectral triple
(A_{\Theta},H_{\Theta},D_{F}).

In the usual left-regular representation, the operator formally has the shape
D_{F,k}=\pi_{\Theta}(k)D_{F}\pi_{\Theta}(k)

on the domain
\operatorname{Dom}(D_{F,k})=\pi_{\Theta}(k)^{-1}\operatorname{Dom}(D_{F}).

Since
\pi_{\Theta}(k)
is bounded, positive, and invertible, the operator

D_{F,k}
is selfadjoint whenever
D_{F}
is selfadjoint. Moreover, compact resolvent is
preserved, because the graph norm of
D_{F,k}
is equivalent, via the bounded
invertible operator
\pi_{\Theta}(k)
, to the graph norm of
D_{F}
. However, because
k
is generally noncentral,
\pi_{\Theta}(k)
does not
commute with
\pi_{\Theta}(a)
. Thus bounded commutators for
D_{F,k}
do not
follow formally from bounded commutators for
D_{F}
. For the usual flat Dirac
operators, they are obtained by the standard conformal pseudodifferential
analysis of noncommutative tori.

There is, however, a simple commutant-side variant which is especially well
adapted to inductive limits. Let
R_{k}\otimes 1_{S}
denote right multiplication by
k
on

L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})\otimes S
. Since
R_{k}
belongs to the commutant
of the left regular representation, it commutes with
\pi_{\Theta}(a)
for all

a\in A_{\Theta}
. For

D_{F,k}^{\mathrm{right}}:=(R_{k}\otimes 1_{S})D_{F}(R_{k}\otimes 1_{S}),\qquad\operatorname{Dom}(D_{F,k}^{\mathrm{right}})=(R_{k}\otimes 1_{S})^{-1}\operatorname{Dom}(D_{F}),

one has, first on the natural core and then by closure,

[D_{F,k}^{\mathrm{right}},\pi_{\Theta}(a)]=(R_{k}\otimes 1_{S})[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(a)](R_{k}\otimes 1_{S}).

Thus bounded commutators are inherited directly from the undeformed triple. Selfadjointness and compact resolvent also follow from the bounded invertible
graph-norm equivalence.

In dimension two, left conformal deformations are the starting point of the
conformal spectral geometry of the noncommutative two-torus; see

[
10
,
20
]
. In higher dimensions, related
Dirac-type operators arise from complex structures and Hermitian metrics on
higher-dimensional noncommutative tori. In particular, the associated
Dolbeault-type operator
\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}^{*}

on
(0,\bullet)
-forms appears in the complex-geometric setting of

[
31
]
.

If one wishes to remain strictly inside the diagonal Fourier-multiplier
framework of Proposition
4.1
, then the
curved or conformal symbol itself must remain diagonal in the Fourier basis. In
that special case one simply checks conditions (a) and (b)
for the new symbol. In general, however, conformal and curved Dirac operators
should be regarded as standard deformations of the basic Fourier-multiplier
triples rather than as direct instances of the proposition.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\Theta\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Psi\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be nondegenerate
skew-symmetric matrices, and let
\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}

be a unital toric map, with
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
, and

z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\mathbb{T}^{m}.

Let
\alpha^{\Theta}\colon\mathbb{T}^{m}\curvearrowright A_{\Theta}
and
\alpha^{\Psi}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\curvearrowright A_{\Psi}

denote the canonical torus actions, and define the continuous group
homomorphism
\beta_{M}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{m},

\beta_{M}(t)_{j}:=\prod_{r=1}^{n}t_{r}^{\,M_{rj}},\qquad 1\leq j\leq m.

Then:
(1)
For every
t\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
,

\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\varphi_{M,z}\circ\alpha_{\beta_{M}(t)}^{\Theta}.

Consequently,
\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta}^{\infty})\subseteq A_{\Psi}^{\infty}.

(2)
For each
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, there exists a scalar
\omega_{M,z}(x)\in\mathbb{T}

such that
\varphi_{M,z}(U^{x})=\omega_{M,z}(x)\,V^{Mx}.

Consequently, the GNS isometry

I_{\varphi}\colon L^{2}(A_{\Theta},\tau_{\Theta})\to L^{2}(A_{\Psi},\tau_{\Psi}),

I_{\varphi}\Lambda_{\Theta}(a):=\Lambda_{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(a)),

sends the Fourier basis vector indexed by
x
to the Fourier basis vector
indexed by
Mx
, up to the phase
\omega_{M,z}(x)
:

I_{\varphi}(\delta_{x}^{\Theta})=\omega_{M,z}(x)\,\delta_{Mx}^{\Psi}.

(3)
For every
1\leq r\leq n
, we have

(11)

\delta_{r}^{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}\,\varphi_{M,z}\circ\delta_{j}^{\Theta}\qquad\text{on }A_{\Theta}^{\infty}.

Equivalently, with
P_{j}=-i\delta_{j}
,
P_{r}^{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}\,\varphi_{M,z}\circ P_{j}^{\Theta}.

If, in addition, one works in the twisted-group basis for

A_{M^{t}\Psi M}
(equivalently, after identifying
A_{\Theta}
with

A_{M^{t}\Psi M}
via the generator identification used elsewhere), then the
phase in
(2)
is exactly
z^{x}:=z_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{x_{m}}.

Proof.

(1) For
1\leq j\leq m
, the toric map is defined by
\varphi_{M,z}(U_{j})=z_{j}V^{Me_{j}}.

Let
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Then

\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(U_{j}))=\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}(z_{j}V^{Me_{j}})=z_{j}\Bigl(\prod_{r=1}^{n}t_{r}^{M_{rj}}\Bigr)V^{Me_{j}}=\varphi_{M,z}\bigl(\beta_{M}(t)_{j}U_{j}\bigr)=\varphi_{M,z}\bigl(\alpha_{\beta_{M}(t)}^{\Theta}(U_{j})\bigr).

Since the generators
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}
generate
A_{\Theta}
, this proves
\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\varphi_{M,z}\circ\alpha_{\beta_{M}(t)}^{\Theta}.

Now let
a\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}
. By definition of the smooth subalgebra, the orbit map
s\longmapsto\alpha_{s}^{\Theta}(a)

is smooth on
\mathbb{T}^{m}
. Since
\beta_{M}\colon\mathbb{T}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{m}
is a smooth group
homomorphism and
\varphi_{M,z}
is continuous, the map

t\longmapsto\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(a))=\varphi_{M,z}(\alpha_{\beta_{M}(t)}^{\Theta}(a))

is smooth on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Hence
\varphi_{M,z}(a)\in A_{\Psi}^{\infty}
, proving

\varphi_{M,z}(A_{\Theta}^{\infty})\subseteq A_{\Psi}^{\infty}.

(2) For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, we have
U^{x}=U_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots U_{m}^{x_{m}},

so

\varphi_{M,z}(U^{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bigl(z_{j}V^{Me_{j}}\bigr)^{x_{j}}.
Write
a_{j}:=Me_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

For ordered monomials in
A_{\Psi}
, one has
V^{a}V^{b}=c_{\Psi}(a,b)\,V^{a+b},
for all

a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},

for a scalar
c_{\Psi}(a,b)\in\mathbb{T}
. Explicitly,

c_{\Psi}(a,b)=\exp\!\Bigl(2\pi i\sum_{1\leq p<q\leq n}a_{q}b_{p}\,\Psi_{p,q}\Bigr).

Thus the product of two ordered monomials is again an ordered monomial, up to
a scalar. Repeating this identity inductively shows that the product
\prod_{j=1}^{m}(V^{a_{j}})^{x_{j}}

is a scalar multiple of the ordered monomial whose exponent is the sum of the
exponents:

\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}a_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}Me_{j}=Mx.

Therefore there exists
\eta_{M}(x)\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\prod_{j=1}^{m}(V^{Me_{j}})^{x_{j}}=\eta_{M}(x)\,V^{Mx}.

Multiplying by the scalar factor
z^{x}:=z_{1}^{x_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{x_{m}}
, we obtain

\varphi_{M,z}(U^{x})=\omega_{M,z}(x)\,V^{Mx}

for some
\omega_{M,z}(x)\in\mathbb{T}
.

Since
\varphi_{M,z}
is unital and both algebras have unique tracial states, we have

\tau_{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\tau_{\Theta}.

Hence the GNS map
I_{\varphi}\Lambda_{\Theta}(a)=\Lambda_{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(a))

is an isometry. Applying this to
a=U^{x}
gives

I_{\varphi}(\delta_{x}^{\Theta})=\Lambda_{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(U^{x}))=\omega_{M,z}(x)\Lambda_{\Psi}(V^{Mx})=\omega_{M,z}(x)\delta_{Mx}^{\Psi}.

(3) For
1\leq r\leq n
, define the
one-parameter subgroup

e_{r}(s):=(1,\ldots,1,e^{2\pi is},1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{T}^{n},\qquad s\in\mathbb{R},

where
e^{2\pi is}
appears in the
r
-th coordinate. By definition of the
canonical derivations,

\delta_{r}^{\Psi}(b)=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\alpha_{e_{r}(s)}^{\Psi}(b),\qquad b\in A_{\Psi}^{\infty}.

Moreover,
\beta_{M}(e_{r}(s))=\bigl(e^{2\pi iM_{r1}s},\ldots,e^{2\pi iM_{rm}s}\bigr)\in\mathbb{T}^{m}.

For a smooth element
a\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}
, consider the smooth orbit map

f_{a}(t):=\alpha_{t}^{\Theta}(a),
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}^{m}.

Then, by the chain rule,

\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}f_{a}\bigl(\beta_{M}(e_{r}(s))\bigr)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}\,\delta_{j}^{\Theta}(a).

Indeed, this identity is immediate on Fourier monomials:

\alpha_{\beta_{M}(e_{r}(s))}^{\Theta}(U^{x})=e^{2\pi is\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}x_{j}}\,U^{x},
whose derivative at
s=0
is
2\pi i\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}x_{j}\Bigr)U^{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}\,\delta_{j}^{\Theta}(U^{x}).

Since
A_{\Theta}^{\infty}
is the space of smooth vectors for the torus action,
the same formula holds for all
a\in A_{\Theta}^{\infty}
.

Now differentiate the equivariance identity
\alpha_{t}^{\Psi}\circ\varphi_{M,z}=\varphi_{M,z}\circ\alpha_{\beta_{M}(t)}^{\Theta}

along the path
t=e_{r}(s)
at
s=0
. This gives

\delta_{r}^{\Psi}(\varphi_{M,z}(a))=\varphi_{M,z}\!\left(\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\alpha_{\beta_{M}(e_{r}(s))}^{\Theta}(a)\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{rj}\,\varphi_{M,z}(\delta_{j}^{\Theta}(a)),

which is exactly (
11
). Multiplying by

-i
gives the corresponding formula for
P=-i\delta
. ∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{M,z}\colon A_{\Theta}\to A_{\Psi}

be a unital toric map, with
M\in M_{n\times m}(\mathbb{Z})
and

z\in\mathbb{T}^{m}.

Assume that
M
has full column rank. Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, and let
F\colon\mathbb{Z}^{m}\to\mathrm{End}(S_{1})_{\mathrm{sa}}
and

G\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathrm{End}(S_{2})_{\mathrm{sa}}

satisfy the two hypotheses of
Proposition
4.1
, and let
D_{F},

D_{G}

be the corresponding Fourier-multiplier Dirac operators.

Write
A_{F}^{\infty}:=A_{\Theta}^{\infty}(D_{F})
and

A_{G}^{\infty}:=A_{\Psi}^{\infty}(D_{G})
, for simplicity.
Define the graph norm on
A_{F}^{\infty}
by

\|a\|_{F}:=\|a\|+\|[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(a)]\|.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\varphi(A_{F}^{\infty})\subseteq A_{G}^{\infty}.

(ii)
The toric map is bounded from the
D_{F}
-graph algebra to the

D_{G}
-graph algebra: there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(12)

\|[D_{G},\pi_{\Psi}(\varphi(a))]\|\leq C\bigl(\|a\|+\|[D_{F},\pi_{\Theta}(a)]\|\bigr)

for every
